A SHIP THAT CUTS THROUGH ICE-PACKS.

One of the leading features of the September "Windsor" is an article on the Russian ice-breaker, the Ermack, a ship that may yet navigate to the Pole, having been specially nonstructed with a view to charg- ing ice-packs. "The Ermack was csmpleted in February of last year. She at once set out for Kronstadt at a season when it would have been ridiculous for any other boat to attempt to approach the frczen Baltic port. The ice was encountered at a distance of 160 miles from Kron- stadt.At first the Ermack went through it readily enough. Her fore propeller sucked the water from beneath the ice, which broke as soon as her bow began to rise upon it, and she was able to maintain a speed of horn four of six knots an hour. But as the more shallow and tresher portions of the Gulf of Finland were approached the task became more difficult. Here the ice, being packed to a thickness of several feet, offered a very great resistance. The Ermack did not fail to break this ice also, but her progress was slow. Sometimes, when she charged the field at full speed, she would advance no more than half a mile before she was brought to a standstill, and then it was necessary to go back and charge again. In other places the wind had broken the ice earlier in the season, and piled it in huge windrows, sometimes extending six feet above the surface of the water and twenty-semen feet below. These ice-banks consisted of separate blocks from two to three feet thick, frozen together into one mass. Against them the Ermack charged with every engine at full pressure. Usually they gave way at the first onslaught, and with a trewiendoua crunching and grind- ing and groaning the stout ship ploughed through walls almost as high as her own sides, leaving behind her an agitated wake of bobbing pieces broken to dimensions of a foot or loss by her powerful screws. At Kronstadt , it had been rumoured that a great ship was approach- ing, cru-hing the ice beneath her. But the inhabitants shook their heads. Such a thing was unheard of—im- possible. They had come to look upon the ice as an irrsfragable barrier. Nevertheless, one day the smoke from the Ermack's funnels disclosed undeniably that she was approaching, and the entire population of Kronstadt turned out to welcome their mid-winter visitor with cheers and jubilation. The Ermack steamed up to the landing, her propellers cruihing and crunching the ice. She turned to port and then to starboard, moved backward as well as forward, treading out a path for herself in every direction. At that time a number of ateamers were imprisoned in the Baltic, and the Ermack proceeded to their rescue. She went first to Revel, and from there conducted thirteen ice-bound steamers to port. One of these was leaking badly at the time of the rescue. But her bow was run into the stern of the Ermack, which is cut away for the special purpose of allowing this to be done, and the disabled 9Pagel was thus conducted safely to port. Altogether the Ermack helped forty-one steamers through the ice during the brief remainder of the winte: season. She had saved to commerce already more than the cost of her construction, and had demonstrated beyond any doubt her commercial utility."

LADIES' LETTER.

MY DEAR NELLIE,

I really begin to think that skirts are not made quite so close-fitting as they were two months ago, for there are several small pleats at the back; but they fasten at the side still, and are unduly long for comfort or cleanliness. Boleros a* no longer considered quite in good taste in thick ma- terials or in fur, and the short, tight-fitting basqued coat, together with the sacque, are far more worn. A new shape, however, is to be found in a coat, which has a rather long basque, rounded in front, flat pockets on the hips, and a very high collar. It is a decidedly smart garment, especially when made in plain deep blue, green, or fawn cloth with velvet collar, lace cravat, and handsome paste or enamel buttons.

Tweeds are now made so light and soft that a costume need not weigh more than a couple of pounds, especially when the skirt is unlined, but worn over a petticoat that matches it in colour in some degree. The skirt is bound with a couple of rows of braid round the short, cutaway coat form the only trimming. Under a little coat may be worn a muslin blouse or font, with a plain black belt to match the tie. Fox ties are, I hear, to be very much worn, and most of them are ornamented with heads and tails. Sable collarettes in natural colours are made in a variety of shapes, a particularlV handsome model having revers and lots of tails on it. A novelty in chinchilla is in the form of a collarette, with bows and long fringed ends of panne velvet in a lovely shade of Wedgwood-blue, and the same model is made in ermine, with bows and ends of black panne velvet.

-Toques of cloth, matching the costume, have kigh loops or twists of velvet, which cross the trim in front and hold fancy quills or curved tail feathers. Big rosettes of tucked or stitched silk is worn on both hats and toques, but the hat most in demand is that with brim projecting far in front, and close and shallow at the back. An equally noteworthy hat, I may tell you is in black silk with gold lace for trimming. I have noticed a still more striking novelty in headgear, where pale blue silk is artfully combined with black tulle and gold galon, the bow of blue silk, which rests -against the hair at the left aide, being finished off with pendant gold ornaments. Toques for the most part turn down on the right hand side and on the left shows off one's coiffure.

Waterproof cloaks should be bought in the autumn, when the new shapes first make their appearance. The modern mackintosh wrap is often a•most stylish and becoming garment, and contrasts very pleasantly with the hideous things produced for feminine wear 10 years ago. Per- haps the most fashionable shape at the moment, is the loose three-quarter length coat, but it is obviously not so useful as the coat that entirely covers the gown. A neat shape is the Chesterfield, but that coat, in which the sleeve is cut in one with the shoulder-piece, is considered smarter. Neither of these coats has a yoke, but a third popular shape is made with a very deep, slightly-pointed yoke, into which the straight full back is set in two box-pleats. Waterproof cloaks are rarely quite long, but are merely large-sized capes reaching to within a few inches of the dress hem. Some are simply finished at the top with small turn-over collars faced with velvet, but the majority have hoods lined with tartan silk. The most faahion- able colours for all descriptions of waterproof Nvrapa are sage-, reseda-, and lichen-green, fawn and drab. Parasols in black and white glace are having a great vogue, and are to be had in many patterns— stripes, checks, and fancy designs. Foulard para- sols with linings of drawn chiffon and bamboo handles are very light to carry, and look par- ticularly nice if made to match foulard gowns, while for seaside use sunshades of coloured linen with muslin frills seem somewhat popular.

Your loving J ENNIE.

"WOMEN'S CHAT."

In the British Army there are no Royal women Colonels- it-Chief, as in Germany, Spain, Italy. and other coun- tries on the Continent. Our own Queen is honorary chief of the Ist Dragoons of the Prussian Guard, and the Duchess of Connaught is hon. colonel of the 64th German Infantry Regiment, but no lady member of the Royal Family occupies such a position in any British Regiment, In Germany these honours are freely bestowed upon the Empress, and various Princesses, but in this country we have only got as far as naming regiments after differ- ent members of the Royal Family. There are seven re- giments in the British Army which are specially named alter the Queen, and there are the same number, seven, which are named after some of our Princesses.

Lord and Lady Roberts, it is hoped, will soon be I returning borne. Lord Kitchener had an immense recep- tion when be returned from the Soudan, and we may be ' sure there will be nothing lacking in the welcome which will be accorded to our veteran Chief, when his task is done. It is interesting to note that a considerable sum has been raised for a presentation to Lord Roberts, horn the women of Ireland. The Marchioness of Londonderry. Lads' Dufferin and Ave, the Countess of Lucan and Belmore, Lady Ashbourne, and Lady Alice Fitzwilliam, have all taken a pr,minent part in the movement, but the subscriptions which are strictly limited from one shilling to one pound, have been received from all classes of the people. It must also not be forgotten that Lady Roberts has done a great deal of good work during her long stay in South Africa. There is an authentic story told in illus- tration of this. When Lady Roberts followed her hue- band to Bloemfontein, at the time of the great rush on the railway, she took seven or eight great trunks with her, and it was remarked that they seemed a little out of place when our sick and wounded were suffering terribly, at the front, for want of ho,pital comforts. Almost needless to say, this was precisely what the trunks contained, Lady Roberts' own baggage being of the smallest proportions. Afterwards it was stated that Lady Roberts was the only person in South Africa, not excepting the Commander-in-Chief himse If, who cou'd have get those trunks through to Bloemfontein at the time.

Sir James Hills-Jchnes, who has recently returned from South Africa,is one of Lord Itt berts' oldest friends and was godfather to the cornmander-in-elief's only son, who was killed at Colenso. There is e remarkable resemblance between the two veteran sol lief's, both in stature and appeararce. and both joined the Royal Artillery in the early fifties. Both went through the Indian Mutiny, and both were present at the siege of Delhi, and in tie operatinos at Lucknow. A-, the cap- ture of Delhi, both were dangerously wounded.and both made almost miraculous recaverit S. So he qopr,tly,they both received the Victoria Cross before the mutiny ended and since then, the friendship between them has been cf that nature which believes that there are no friends like old friends. The " Khaki" crt. Z 3 has completely "died cut." It has not even lasted until the return of the troops from South Africa, and in fact, it never "took on" to the extent that manufacturers and tradesfolk anticipated and prepared for. There was really little or nothing in its various forms that was pretty or becoming, and this consideration, which appeals with irresistible force to womenkind, soon got the better of the patriotic fervour, which was aroused during the earlier stages of the war.

Those of us who secured some of the numberless bargains offered at the July sales, have had ample opportunity for getting full value for our enoney,as €arly autumn toilettes have scarcely yes been needed. Rarely has the month of September seen us donning such airy fairy attire, transparent yolkes and sleeves indeed, being the rule, rather than the exception. The transparent idea, however, is a very risky one. In the height of summer it spoils our skin,and is really not by any means as cool as it lroks, and at this season of the year, the danger of a serious chill is considerable. It is a very simple matter to attach a soft, light lining of some description, and the sensible among us will se treat all bodices built " transparentwise." The inclination of skirts to flow out round the feet is more pronounced than ever. This state of affairs is brought about by means of flounces. Flounces cut on the straight, or crosswise, flounces narrow or wide, flounces pleated, gathered, or tucked. All this fulness only commences below the knees. Above, our skirts, hether pleated or tucked or plain remain almost as sheath-like as ever, the only difference consisang in the box pleat uhich now invariably finishes off the back. Such is the enormous demand for false bair, that it is said as much as 150 tons of human hair is made up every year in Paris alone. Most of this comes from China, and—enquiries would probablylreveal the further information—frcm people who have no further use for it in this world. Large quantities are also gathered in France, where thlre are regular fairs for this purpose, and a good deal comes from Germany, Italy and Spain. There are numbers of dealers wbo attend the fies in the country towns and villages, and who induce the women folk to exchange their tresses for some piece of finery, generally of a gaudy description, and of very paltry value. The tondenr r pioussaire, as the buyer is called, always has the best of the bargain, and for some hair he can easily obtain very high prices. For long white, and naturally curly hair,as much as £2OO or £3OO per pound, is attainable. A new Chinese Hand Laundry has been started in London, which, by all accounts, is going to do wonders to improve our washing and ironing arrangements, Already nearly 200 Chinamen are at work, but Lew pre- mises, covering some 17 acres of ground, on the out- skirts of London, are in course of erection where over a thousand Chinese laundrymen will be employed by the end of the year. Everyone knows that the Chinaman has an excellent reputation for laundry work, and Lon- doners will welcome his appearance in a field of domestic necessity, in which there is much room for improve- ment. _ .

The ChIOCSA do not pretend to charge lower rates but they daclare that they use no chemicals and do not effect the destruction of linen which is such a conspicu- ous feature of the modern machine laundry. There is perhaps same objection to importing large numbers of Chinamen, and in America, Canada, and Australia; they adopt stringent meass to keep them out because they are so succeslful in competing with hand labour. But no such consideration will prevent us welcoming the Chinese laundrymen in their thousands, if only they can do away with the annoyance and vexation which we are constAntly experiencing ovcr the returned b:,slset of frayed and destroyed linen. M. Edward Lloyd's farewell t ur of the great towns of England and Scotland will be an event of extreme interest to the musical world. B.gioning on October Bth at Hull,it will end on December 10th at Birmingham, and the distinguished tenor will then return into private life. Meanwhile it is unfortunate that, for the present at any rate, there is no one who can quite fill the place which has been occupied during so many years by Mr. Lloyd. His retirement affords a splendid opportunity for any really great tenor to come to the front, and no doubt in time the vacancy will be filled, but so far there is no clear indication that the need has found the man.

BLI,CIEBERRY JAH.—To every pound of berries add three-quartets of a pound of sugar, cook slowly till the berries are well done, stirring otten, to prevent burning. A little lemon peel added, will improve the flavour.

"All who know Christ belong to the true Church whether Catholic or Protestant," replied the girl. "The true Church is the Spirit of Christ reigning in our lives."

"Who told yon this?" " God." "But think, my child. Christ came to establish a Church. You believe that?"

"Yes." "Well, where is it except in the Catholic Church ? "

"It is everywhere, where there is love, and faith, and obedience," said Alizon. "It is not narrowed by sects or boundaries of any sort. It embraces all those who love Christ and accept Him as their Saviour."

"Let me shew you where you are wrong," said the priest. "You may adduce arguments which I cannot meet with argument," answered the girl, "for y' are cleverer than I. But—l know it seems boaok,fu, —I have knowledge which has not come to me through the intellect, and I know that the Church of Christ is larger than the Roman Church, and that in claiming to be the only Church, it limits God, it resolves religion into a mere sect. Chris- tianity is a spirit, a great motive power in the soul; it is Christ living in us." "And how came you by this knowledge?" asked the priest. "I do not know," replied Alizon, reverently. "I feel like the blind lad must have felt to whom our Lord gave his sight, and who answered Christ's enemies by saying: One thing I know, that whereas I was blind, now I see.'

"My child," he said, "while this seems very plausible and beautiful, it is in reality full of deceit. God has appointed certain means whereby men must be saved. what means is expressed in the Church." \

"Who said so?" said Alizon, quickly. "Give me our Lord's authority for saying this?" "That could be easily given," said the priest, "but you would not understand. It is for you to obey those whom God hath appointed. And let me say this," here Ritzoom's voice became rather angry, "it is no light matter for a child of the Church to disobey her teaching." "Will you explain?"

"I mean this: Protestants may be forgiven, for their sin is that of ignorance ; they have been robbed of religion, they have not been reared in the light of truth; but for one who has known the truth, who has lived amidst all the advantages of the Church, who has received her sacraments, and yet who becomes apostate, I know of no forgiveness." "Then you mean to say-" "I mean to say that your position is terrible," said Ritzoorn, "more terrible than you can think. Reflect, my child, reflect." "I have reflected," said Alizon: "and God has told me that your words are false. What! Do you mean to say that God will punish me for ever for following His own leadings, for being true to what I believe to be the truth, for obeying the commands of our Lord? Father Ritzoorn, whatever I once believed, I could not believe it now! It is repugnant to everything high and holy." 'Nevertheless, it is true.

"When you can make me believe that the sun causes darkness, you may persuade me to accept that," said the girl. "No, I do not fear the threats of the Church. All that is gone! " Again Ritzoom felt baffled. "If you will not yield to the pleadings of the Church, I assume that you are not dead to love, and to filial duty," he said. "Do you know what you are doing? You are breaking your mother's heart, you are blighting her life. Does your new religion teach you to do that? You know that she is not strong, and if you persist in your madness, I fear the worst; you will be guilty of your mother's death."

Ritzoom had fixed his arrow at last. Alizon burst out sobbing; and seeing his advantage Ritzoom pressed it. He pictured Lady Neville 's joy if they were again united in sympathy and faith, he told of her agony if she, Alizon, should persist in her terrible course. Perhaps he was not wise in saying so much, for presently Alizon's eyes flashed defiance.

"Father Ritzoom," she said, "suppose my mother was a Protestant instead of a Catholic, and had the same feelings towards Catholicism as she has towards Protestantism; and suppose that I, het daughter, was converted to Catholicism, would you have me remain a Protestant, because by becoming a Catholic, 1 broke her heart ?"

"That—that would be different," stammered Ritzoom. "Surely you cannot place the—" "Excuse me," said the girl, interrupting him fot the first time, "it is no use, I love my mother more than words can say; but I must be true to my convictions, I must be true to my God." "And this man ? " said Ritzoom, "this man whc has been the means of leading you into error, will you persist in your madness concerning him ? " "Father Ritzoom," said Alizon, "for the sake of what I once believed, I have told you of the new life which has come to me. But I decline to speak with you concerning Mr. Rutland. Good-night." _ .

The Jesuit sat for a long time in perfect -still- ness. Presently a look of conviction came into hie eyes. He hal thought of a new method of action. "Yes; that will do it," he said, with a smile.

CHAPTER XXX. RUTLAND'S HOME-COMING.

"What we need, Lady Neville, is time."

Her ladyship looked doubtful as Ititzoom spoke. She had come to see, in spite of the Jesuit'r assurances, that he had not truly estimated her daughter's character, had not realised that hex whole life had been completely changed:.

"You may be right; b-ut wliat then ? " "With time I believe everything will come right, only--" he hesitated a second. "Only what? " _ _ _

"Well, we n;aist understand the case, and face it boldly. Your daughter has doubtless become unsettled."

113! "Yes," admitted her ladyship with a sigh. "And she is in love with this—Rutland.." Lady Neville was silent. "What we must do at all hazards is to restore your daughter to the faith." "Oh, if we can!"

"And, of course, we must destroy all possibility of a marriage between the two."

"Time! but how will time help? Alizon will soon be of age, and she will do whatever she believes to be right."

"And- she loves this man? I mean, do you think it will last ? "

"I am afraid it will. She is no giddy child, an she has never sliewn the slightest preference fot any man before. And—why, think of it! If Alizon were to marry this Protestant minister here in Lynford, I—l think I should go mad! The papers would be full of it, the town would reek with gossip. Besides, think of it—my only child, a dissenting minister's wife!"

"It would be terrible, terrible !" said Ritzoom, like one musing. "Still I have hopes." "But do not buoy me up with false hopes," cried her ladyship. "Yon see, she went on, excitedly, "you thought her change of faith was only a passing whim, and that you could soon bring her to reason. But you were mistaken." "I do not admit that," said Ritznom, " he hesitated for a second. "What is your real opinion of Rutland ? " he added. "I have nothing against him personally, He is well conducted, and gentlemanly. But for one episode, which, I suppose, he could defend, he tias acted honourably." "You know nothing of his antecedents?" "Nothing except what he has told me," and Lady Neville related the purport of what Duncan nad said about himself months before.

"And I desire none, Lady Neville ; still it seems to me our battle must be fought through him." "How ? " "He struck me as having a high sense of duty." "I should say he would try to do what he believed to he right."

"Do you think he would do a great deal to win your consent for Alizon to be his wife?"

THE TEMPTATION OF DUNCAN RUTLAND.

BY JOSEPH HOCKING,

Author of "The Birthright," "Mistress Nancy Molesworth," "All Men are Liars," "The Story of Andrew Fairfax," &c.

CHAPTER XXIX. THE PRIEST AND THE WOMAN.

When Ritzoom and Alizon were left together, the former looked somewhat ill at ease.

"Well, you have escaped a good deal of our English winter, my child," he said, quietly. "I had hoped to have gone to Rome myself this winter, but the fates forbade it. Besides, I was doubtless doing better work in England. And, after all, nothing gives one so much satisfaction as hard work. Sometimes when lam overworked, I think I should like to take my ease, but that is only for a brief time."

"-Alizon simply nodde6l. She knew that she had not been asked to meet him to hear this, and she wished that he would at once approach the subject which brought them together. But Ritzoom was too wise to do this, even although he guessed what was passing in Alizon's mind. He knew that his first business was to try and create a certain atmosphere, and to impress the girl with his presence. And so for the next few minutes he talked pleasantly about some of his experiences in Rome, and in other parts of the world.

She felt, too, that he was drawing the net of the conversation slowly around her, and by subtle inference made her feel the heinousness of opposing the will of God as expressed in His Church. The atmosphere became more and more charged with priestly influence, and he impressed her with the idea that be spoke not simply as a man, but as an ambassador of a great mystical power. She could not explain why, but he made the last few weeks as a dream, he aroused old influences, old memories. He made her think of the long line of Nevilles on her father's side of her house, who had been true to the ancient faith, and of the equally long line of Bartons, of which her mother was a descendant, who had all been faithful to the Church. She remembered, moreover, the boast of both the Nevilles and of the Bartons, that they, through the stress of the centuries, in spite of changes of Kings and Governments, in spite of war and perse- cution, had never married into Protestant families, never changed in their attitude towards religion. Ritzoorn said nothing about these things, but he made her feel them, nevertheless ; and thus the influences of heredity, of association and history, became working forces in the girl's life. Thus, at length, it seemed natural that he should refer to the plans to which she had been a party, as though she were still interested in winning a man who had been a dangerous antagonist to the servants of God, to become a champion of the true faith.

"It seems we have not been altogether successful, my child," he said, confidentially, keeping his eyes fixed upon her. "Well, that is all the more reason for renewed effort, for unflagging service in the cause of truth.

He did not seem to regard her own change as worthy of notice; rather he seemed to look upon her as faithful to her old ideals, and of being filled with the longings which possessed her six months befoi e.

Aiizon did not answer; indeed, at that moment she could not. He seemed to place a weight upon her lips and upon her heart. "You were with Mr. Rutland a good deal in Rome ? " he asked.

"Yes ; but scarcely ever alone. Father Matthew accompanied us on the few occasions on which I went sight-seeing with him." "Oh, yes, I suppose so. And yet you must have noticed how he was impressed, you must have noticed how Rome influenced him?" "Yes." "Of course you did. Well, it appears that up to the present he has not yielded to the truth, and grace has not yet touched his heart. What would you advise now? What can we do to save his soca, and make him a champion for the truth? It will be a glorious victory, will it not ? " and Ritzoom's voice became, if possible, more gentle, and for the first time Alizon noticed a tone of pleading. "You do not appear to understand," she said, as if struggling with herself. "Oh, yes, I think I do," he replied. "I know there are difficulties, but, as you know, we never give up hope. We go on year after year, decade after decade. Besides, you have great influence over him. You must have. Well, it is for you to use it for God and His Church."

"Mr. Rutland will never be converted to your faith," stammered Alizon.

"You should never say that, my child. Your fathers never gave up hope even in the darkest hours. Besides, the greater the difficulties the greater the reward." "But, but--" Alizon struggled to say. "We never admit of buts, ' " interrupted Ritzoom, with a smile, "never. We all had before us a glorious ideal. We arranged to fight a bloodless battle. We determined to win over a dangerous enemy and make him a friend. Well, so far we have failed; but we must not give up. That would be cowardly, unworthy of the children of the Church. And, my child," here Ritzoom's voice became more solemn, more impressive, "God expects it of you. He is looking on us now. lie is pleading with you, and yet commanding you never to turn aside from such a glorious object. And it is a terrible terrible ! thing to disobey God." For a moment Alizon fe4 almost overcome. The man, more than his words, made her as weak as a child. She almost lost her mental balance, and a great longing came into her heart to fall on her knees before him and beg forgiveness. It seemed as though her very life hung in the balance, and as though priestly power and the influence of past centuries would be greater than all else. It was an awful battle that she fought, all the more awful because her strength seemed gone, and because the very foundations of her life were shaken. Ritzoom saw his advantage, and he kept his eyes fixed steadily upon her. And thus Alizon stood shivering on the brink of begging forgiveness for what seemed at that moment a sin; she appeared about to yield to the influences which were around her. Then her strong vigorous nature asserted itself, the life-blood of her intellect began to flow, the intuitions of her soul were made manifest. She rose to her feet, and as she did so a great weight seemed to roll from her. "It is no use my keeping back what you perhaps already know," she said, firmly. "Mr. Rutland will never become converted to your faith; and I cannot assist you in such an object, because I no longer believe in those things which I formerly held fast."

---"You cannot mean this, my child," said Ritzoom, like one surprised. "I do mean it," replied Alizon, strength and conviction coming back with every word she spoke. "Religion is no longer what it was to me." "If what you say is true, it cannot be," said Ritzoom. "But I cannot believe it; you are a child of the Church, and you cannot give up truth for error. You cannot leave that one true Church to become a mere sectarian."

"No, I do not leave the Church," replied Alizon ; "rather, 1 more truly enter . .

_ . "My child, what you say is impossible. There is no Church outside the Holy Roman Church. Where is this Church that you speak about?" "It is in my heart., my life," cried Alizon. "This is foolishness," replied Ritzoom. "What you say is but the groundless phraseology of heretics.'

"It is not groundless," cried the girl. All fear was gone now, and she spoke freely. "What is your authority?" asked the priest. "Our Lord is my authority," replied Alizon. "'The Kingdom of God is within you,' He said." "Alt, I see!" and there was a touch of asperity in his voice, "you have been reading the New Test ament. " "I have been reading but little else for a long time," cried the girl. "It is one of the most profound books in the world," said Ritzoom ; "but it' cannot be inter- preted except by God's chosen ones. You are but a child, and would you pretend to know its meaning more truly than the elect of the Church who have given it to the world, and who have been studying it for eighteen hundred years? " "Our Lord said that God hid many things from the wise and the prudent and revealed them unto babes," said the girl. "But what can you know of the New Testament, its history, and its deepest meaning?" asked the priest. "I know enouzh to read and to Dray." she

"Will you allow me to manage everything? Tosay what I think wise, to make promises in your name ?' "What promises? " "I do not know, but only such promises as may be for your happiness, for Alizon's eternal ealva- tion, and for the glory of our Church. Will you trust me fully? " Lady Neville was silent. "Have you sufficient confidence in me, to give me an entirely free hand to deal with Rutland ?" "But he is not in England." "He soon will be. I see that the Tudor congre- gation have arranged to give him a welcome home the day after to-morrow. Unless we are careful they will soon be arranging to give a welcome to Alizon as the minister's wife."

"Oh, do something, Father Ilitzoom " "You trust me, then, trust me fully. You give me authority to act in your name?" "Yes, yes; but remember that Alizon is my only child, that she is as dear as my life to me.' "I will remember that."

A little later Ritzoom Father Sheen walked back to the latter's house. As they passed Tudor chapel Ritzoom read the bill announcing Rutland's home-coming very carefully, but he spoke no word to his companion. "Have you done anything—settled anything ? " asked the simple old parish priest again and again as they walked homeward, but Ritzoom made no reply; he was thinking deeply of the task which lay before him. Throughout the whole of the next day he did not leave the house, neither did he exchange con- fidences with his brother priest. He had work to do that was both delicate and difficult, and he determined to take no false steps. "The initial difficulties will be the greatest," he concluded, after formulating his plans. "If I can once overcome them, I can begin to weave my web around him, and then--" "It must be done, too," he continued thinking after pacing the room for some time. "The child must be saved from such a fatal step, and the Church must be defended. Yes, that's it. I feel like Cardinal Wiseman in that respect; I never cared for anything but the Church, and for it I would gladly give my life. But I wonder how it is that Alizon, a child of many generations of faithful Catholics, should, should—but there! I must heal the breach, I must heal the breach!" On the Saturday evening Ritzoom wended his way towards Tudor Chapel. The huge building was filled with people, even although it wanted ten minutes before the time announced for the meeting to commence. On nearly every face was a look of pleasing expectation, and all eyes were turned towards the vestry door. All around the priest men and women were talking in subdued tones but load enough for him to understand their convergation. He found that Rutland was their sole topic. Ritzoom looked at the vestry door and saw Rutland come into the chapel and walk up the rostrum stairs, accompanied by some of the officers of the church. When the young minister appeared the whole audience, numbering nearly two thousand people, rose en masse, to cheer him. As it was afterwards remarked, never was such a sight seen in Lynford before. A great wave of emotion passed over the great congregation ; old men with weather-beaten faces wiped away the tears which they could not suppress; old motherly women shouted out their welcome; young men and maidens cheered lustily, while all over the great church were heard expressions of gladness and thankfulness.

The meeting was an utter surprise to the young man. He knew nothing about it until a few hours before it commenced, and even then he had no idea that it would assume such dimensions. In the space before the communion-rails he saw a large piece of cloth, which was evidently placed there to cover some things beneath, but he had no idea what it was. Everything was surprising, over- whelming. As the crowd welcomed him he felt so touched, so overcome by the manifestation of their love, that the tears welled up into his eyes, and a sob rose in his throat which could not be suppressed. The senior church officer gave out a hymn, in which the great congregation joined. How the sound rose and swelled! Perhaps there are few parts of the country which are more noted for singing than Lancashire, and as nearly two thousand voices joined in the grand old hymn: "All hail the power of Jesu's Name!" it was difficult to describe the impression made. To Ritzoom it came as a revelation. He did not understand the mental and spiritual outlook of the people, he did not realise the religious convictions of the sturdy sons of the North. Here were people who had cast aside all priestly claims, and who yielded to no religious authority, save that of Christ Himself, and yet they were loyal to His ambassador. Here were men and women who con- fessed their sins to none but God, who trusted only to Him for the consciousness of forgiveness, and yet they rejoiced in their faith, and had no doubts about its reality. They claimed perfect liberty, mental and religious, and yet they were sober, law- abiding, God-fearing people. It was such as they who had made the nation great, and it was such as they who made it impossible for the priest to gain any footing in the country. Throughout the meeting he sat listening and taking note of everything. He heard the old gray- headed leaders of the church, as well as the young men just beginning to bear the burdens of life, testify to their affection for Rutland, and welcome him back with heart-felt warmth. He heard them praise him for upholding the faith, and defending the liberties of the people. He listened to their prayers as they pleaded for his welfare—bodily, mentally, spirtually. "The Church has an impossible task," he thought, sadly. "These Nonconformists form more than half of the religious life of the country, and they will never yield. Presently a presentation was made. A writing- desk, a large parcel of valuable books, a gold watch and chain, and other articles were given with the Church's love, and the people's prayers. "I have said that these things are given by Tudor Church," said the chairman, who happened to be Mayor of the town, "but I am not quite correct. People from almost every church asked to con- tribute. Even Catholics, or those who used to be Catholics, are among the contributors. lam glad of this. It shews that the Church is broader than we think, and it shews how strongly our friend and minister has laid hold of our town."

When Rutland got up to reply, there was the wildest enthusiasm. For several minutes he was unable to speak, and, eventually, when the audience settled down to listen, his emotion almost overcame him. That great sea of kindly faces, those tear-dimmed eyes, those honest cries: "God bless tha' lad!" made speech almost impossible. He mastered himself presently, however, and was able to speak freely. He thanked them for their kindness, their generosity, their prayers, their love, and expressed the hope that he should in the coming days be able to prove himself worthy of it. He would not describe his experiences in Rome; perhaps, sometime soon, he would be able to give them a lecture, when he would be able to speak about them at length. And yet there was one thing he would like to mention in connection with Rome, which was in some degree associated with the controversy in which he took part some months before.

"And it is suggested by the chairman's 'speech about the Church.' "he said. "We Free Church- men are often sneered at because of our divisions, and at one time it was a painful problem to me. But the real meaning of Church has become more and more impressed upon me. As you know, the Romanists claim that theirs is the only Church, and they rest their claim upon certain so-called facts in history, and a supposed long line of Apostolical succession. It is a very sad fact that a great mass of the young and educated life of Italy is drifting from religion altogether, and that Romanisni fails, even at its headquarters, to meet the intellectual and spiritual life to young free Ray. At the same time, great hosts of the people belong to the Church—the real Church; because they love Christ, and are trying to be true to Him. On my way home I stopped at Berlin, where there is a strong Protestant spirit; here, again, in spite of ignorance, unbelief, sin, there is a great multi- tude belonging to the true Church—the Church of love and obedience to Christ. And the same is true here in Lynford. We have our different denominations, but we belong to the same Church, in so far as we love and serve our Lord._

"I know this seems like a platitude, but it has been made real to me during these last three months. Moreover, the great fact that the true Church of Christ is one has been made real. Of course, all those pagan trappings of priestcraft are foolishness, all that talk about the Church being confined to one organisation is worse than foolishness, but the oneness remains; it is a spirit

of love anct oheatence, ana tnat, manic tiod, spite of ignorance, bigotry, and error, belongs to all the Churches. Aud* this makes the Church SO much greater than we imagine; it oversteps all the barriers of men, it embraces all pure, loving hearts,, it makes the Church united, and it makes iF universal. I shall continue to light what I believe to be error. I shall still try Liad destroy ell cramp..ng, narrowing influences; b..t I shall more than ever recognise the fact that the Spirit of Christ is everywhere, and wherever He is, the Church is. Indeed, I like to think of the words of that grand old father in the early Church, mean St. Ignatius. He, when writing to the Smyrnaeans, said: Wherever Jesus Christ mei be, there is the Catholic Church.' " His words struck a new note in the meaning, an Ritzoom felt its power. It made the work he ha, set himself to accomplish more difficult still, be he did not deviate from his purpose. It was very late before the meeting broke up, f°r Rutland had to diake hands with hundreds of people, but Ritzoom stayed to the end. When the building was nearly empty, he made his way Rutland's side. "Let me also welcome you back," he said, it a smile. "I have been much struck with the' gathering, and with your address, and I should like an hour's chat with you about it, and about other things.' "You are very kind," said Rutland; "but I arl) afraid I could not see you until to-morrow eight" after my work is over. "That will do spendidly. Shall I call at your lodgings about nine o'clock ? A SCHOOL BOARD.

Address by Mr. F. Monks.

Strong Indictment of Voluntary Schools.

Silver-street Day School anniversary services Were held on Sunday in Bewsey-road Wesleyan 'Chapel. The preacher at the morning service was ....the Rev H G Roberts, M.A , and at night, the Rev ttehry Gulliford, of Mysore. occupied the pulpit. lo the afternoon, a musical service was held and Was presided over by Mr James Dodds. There was Only a moderate attendance. The school children were accommodated on an improvised platform and Sang a number of hymns under the conductorship %of Mr Upton. Solos were sung by Mrs Stone and tlr Baker. Mr Wadsworth presided at the organ. The Chairman in the course of a brief address re- erred to the high pesition occupied by the schoolmaster in the national life and he was sure they were all very clad to help the echools of their country when they had -it in their power. He was one of the remnant of that Class of men in Warrington who, twenty years ago, Would have liked to have had a school board in Warring- ton. Toe denominational schools then hold the field as they held it now, and the only thing they could do was ,to help those schools which came nearest the idea they desired to see established, and he believed they had Nola schools in Warrington. Silver-street Wesleyan School was one of them. There was no attempt made .!,0 teach dogma ; no attempt to push Wesleyan Method- lehl forward in connection with the teaching of the , I.lchool, but a sincere desire to bring up the children ititellectually and morally without any approach to 'dogmatic teaching. The present government had passed an Act which enabled them to give a 'Certain aid to voluntary schoels, but they had a Very wide discretion and the managers of Silver-street Schools had received a warning that unless the volun- tary subscriptions were increased there was a probe bility that the aid grant would not be paid at all. There Were many people who ()fleeted to Board schools who 'were also unwilling to help voluntary schools. There Was much talk about the cost of education in Board Schools when the matter was under discussion twenty Years ago in Warringtoe but what was the cost when compared with the rich harvest of men who would have been better equipped for the various fields of work in the town, than they were to day. Toe cost would have been far more than returned in the better equipment of Children who would new have been men in the works Where education was So eeeential. Unless the increased ,enbscriptions were forthcoming, the managers would ztave ,to let the Elucation Department take up the nutter of education, themselves." Mr. MosKs then delivered an address on "Elementary tiucetiou " He said The qu-stion of elementary education is one of such importance that it has always received the most careful attention by the hest minds oE the nation. The early foundations of our grammar Schools, the magnificent gifts to our public echools and 4niversities bear testimony to the anxiety of our 'Countrymen huldreds of years ago to provide for the education of the young, and all through the centuries knee then there has been rnech time, thought, and koney spent upon its development. It is difficult to ticderstand why it is that we, have not a better and more Perfect system than we have. But when you look back tIPoO the history of the Church and its polity, and look tsere closely into the management of our educational !thdowmeeta it is not hard te fin 1 the cause. Toe selfish interests of the parson and the equire, the indifference the the rich to the needs of the poor, the powerlessness of poor to help themselves, are all contribiring causes Zo a atate of things we cannot defend, and it required the strong hand of the reformer, backed up by voluntary effort, to lay the foundation of our present imperfect lb/stem. Even now we have the clerical influence against Board Schools' which opposes the introduction of any .heYstem which does net practicaliy leave control in the 4xlllds of the clergy. So jealous are they of losing their influence over the minds and censciences of their People that they prefer to continue a system admittedly inefficient for the effective Purposes 21 elementary education, rather than leave them the care of sensible laymen, who ar9 Ore concerned for the children's real good than for the bolstering up of denominational interests. which Ire always able to take care of themselves. The pre- system at be‘t is one of isolated action, as every lichee' is fighting for its own hand, and making every Feffort to make ends meet without the aid of voluntary (ibscriptions. To earn the grant every effort is put Orth to earn as much as possible. Free places have to ,eue provided for a class of children when', unsuited aseoeiate with the better class of children, whose Parents are anxious to give !Ir en every advantage and gee from the demoralising influences of children /trheise standard of morality and intelligent converse- are taken from same of the worst slams in our large ZoWns. Under present conditions, so far as Waraington concerned no provision is made for this class of e;4ldren, but all are taught together, the dullard with 8 quick and intelligent, and no adaptation of the _caching to the special neede of the school. Is this just d right to the deserving poor, who cannot prevent it led are powerless to help themselves ? With the School Inspector on one hand enforcing atttndance under a Density, and narrow means on the other hand. which Prevents them sending their children to a higher class (school, parents are forced to accept the condi:ions they do not like, and submit to an injustice they cannot pre- vent. Yet all this is done under the plea of giving a religious education. I venture to affirm that half-a- lozen utter children let loose into any schcol will do more to loWer the tone of the school in a week than your so-called teligious instruction will correct in a month. It is all Ver7 well for educated and well-to-do people to talk about teligions e iucatien in our present denominational schools Ut they will not send their own children te teem) and ;i',ctild be mortally offended at anyone who suggested it. by then do they oppose the introduction of a better 'Ffiteem, which at least will make an honest attempt to :Ivo to the honest poor, something approaching to the ,atne advantages which they claim for their own children cbrough the instrumentality of those educational endow- Tll, r!llte which have come down to us from the past? I am `te° Pessimist, hut I cannot go in and out among you, and eee the cases that come before us at the Police Court, Without being struck at the growing lawlessness of young people, and asking myself what is ,"tle cause ? We may repress by the forces of law and order, but repression is no effective substi- eate for that self-respecting obedience to the law which the only safeguard for the freedom of all our institu• i'ions. Then, again, the methods, the appliances, and ‘he hygienic conditions in schools isolated from each Other cannot be as effective in their results as under a Olore thorough, comprehensive, and well-equipped sys- tem administered with the single object of tne children's Roed. You may tell nee, as some do, that the present 37stem is a great improvement upon that of our fathers, Intl that in the main we get fairly good results from it, ..hat after all we must have some hewers of wood and awera of water,or we shall have to do it for ourselves. well, God has given us bountifully of His rich gifts, ithout being any respecter of persons or place. I fail `o see why we should not do our best to make them aCcessible to all His children, rich and poor alik 3. All :Will admit that :as regards mental capacity. intellectual ,cedowments, moral and spiritual gifts, He bestows 'hese to both rich and poor alike, melting no distinction. iiat is it not a duty which the rich man owes to the poor hat his children shall have as good an opportunity as Detsible to get that mental training which is so neces- litary in the struggle of lire?—that his children shall have ,he best opportunities for developing the intellectual ,aculties with which they may be endowed, and that .Ihe moral and spiritual gifts of their nature shall have ‘-heir free expression under as favourable conditions as Possible? In our giving don't let us give the children a 'etone when tiny ask for bread, and try to persuade our- rielyes that we have done our duty to them- lam afraid however, that the popular idea of elementary education emistakes the means for the end, that in some crude 'ashion they have the idea that when a child can read and write, can work some ( xercisos in arithmetic, to qet as far as the Rule of Three and Practice, with a little Drawing.and a smattering of some Science subject, -7llat he is educated and fit to take an important position na the world to start with ; and parents seem perfectly astonished if you complain of the want of grasp and 'cental capacity to do the work required of their rhil Open. Now, so long as this idea remains, the great bulk °I the people' will remain content with the present or aly system which will show fairly satisf.,ctory reports .1f an examination by an inspector. when the best of our children have passed the purely elementary sage of their education we knew inlet their education in the true sense of the werd is only beginning I, therefore, rejoice that through the instrumentality ot the continuation schools and toe technical sch o 1 prey'. sion is made for any chdd, boy or girl, Co continue his or her educetion in the subjects which more nearly concern their ordinary pursuits of life, and also in the special subjects which may have a direct bearing upon tee business in which they may be engaged, or pursuits in hich they may feel a special interest. Alteouge for a time the work may be hindered by the want of proper accommoiation, and proper appliances for carrying on the work as efficiently as we could with, I hope the time will come before lone when these defects will be re nedied by the building of new techuic.st schools, which will enable the youth of the town to pursue their studies with all the advantages that experience,orgenis- ation, and wealth can provide. It is a work in which all can unite with earnestness and enthusia-m to attain the object in view. My regret is that we cannot deal with the elementary education on the same lines of independent action, establishing infant schools in all parts of the town, and supp y a teaching staffs equal to their needs, furniseed with all the best appliances for the work ; then graded schools for the older and more advanced children, where merit, good behaviour and character wood find its due recognition, and be passed into the secondary school with specialized instruction to meet the higher branches of education. At the same time we should establish schools for the more neglected children who from various causes would require, at least for a time, more care and attention from the teaching staff of a somewhat different character to the rest. Soma would complain of the cost, but all elucetion worth the name is costly at first, because of the care and attention that is required from the teaching staff, which ought to be nest effective and consequently well paid. Still, as I have already said, there is the duty of the rich to the poor, and the State to its own future members. In imerca and on the Continent, they are most liberal in this matter of primary education, and with the best results. Switzerland, where the rich and poor are educated together, is, perhaps, the best and most highly educated country in the world. Certainly its technical schools are the best and most highly orgaisise Laud what is the result ? Notwithstanding their insular position and the absence of such mineral wealth as we possess, they are successfully holding their own in the industrial world and as mechanical and electrical eegineers they are in the very front rank. Is regards culture, self- respect and obedience to the laws they are singularly fortunate in this respect, and I venture to say that you may travel from one end of Switzerland to the other and not hear anything like the coarse and filthy language that you hear in many of the back slums of our large towns. But I have another reason for the regret that we cannot deal with elementary schools on broader lines. I believe it is very desirable that we should so stimulate the reasoning faculties of our children that they begin to think f3r themselves as early as possible. To do this the system of cramming a child's mind with what it does not understand must be abandoned, and more care and attention given to object lessons, and the methods for interesting a child, not only in its lessons but in the things about it, drawing out the faculties of the child's mind in such a way as to make it think about what it is doing and why ? I believe such a system of teaching will do more than any amount of what is called religious teaching in helping a child to distinguish between right and wrong. When once you get a child to discriminate, and to do that which is right because the child itself sees it is the right, you at once bring into active play the couse.ence, which is the most powerful influence in the religious training of a child. With a quickened conscience and the stimulus of intelligent interest in what it does lies the greatest possibility of the real education of a child, and you lay the foundation of its future success. You may say that a child is too young to understand. Don't make that most fatal of all mistakes in underrating what a child can do. Go back upon your own experi- ence, and try to ascertain how young you were when you could not understand. Children do understand often before they can walk, certainly before they can talk,and it only needs you to talk intelligently to them to find out how much they do understand and how intelligent they really are. Don't they often ask questions you can- not answer, or don't care to answer, and do you for a moment suppose the question is not the result of the child's attempt at reasoning for itself ? The rich and well-to-do don't fail to take advantage of this, and you often find children of tender years not only intelligent beyond their years, but with a will power which speaks well for their future self control. These are the faculties you require in the world of struggle and etrife. He who cannot reason for himself and decide what is the right and tbe wrong, or echo has not the will power to control his own action, is the you'll vale lies at the mercy of circumstance and its environment. He will drift the stream, and it altogether depends upon where he is in the stream, whether he goes on down the river of life or is caught upon the whirlpools or eddies which chs:k and retard his progress and ultimately throw him stranded on the shore a hopeless wreck. To him who knows the stream,and is determined to keep clear ef its danger, is given the sense of enjoyment and the pleasure of success as the result of his effort, and who shall say what are or are not the possibilities of his or her future ? To the man of right principle, of intelligent insight, with firmness of character to steadily pursue his aim in life, opportunities of useful- ness are ever crossing his path. This is not the time or place for advice to the young. We are met to-day , to do the best we can under the conditions which are alone possible for the present ; but if the evils of which • I have spoken can be remedied to any extent ' whatever, it is our duty to remedy them. The responsibility for sin present imperfect system , of primary education rests with these who actively and persistently do their bast to prevent a better being established, and whatever may be their motive, if it is anything short of an earnest desire to do the best for oar young children it is an unworthy one and ought to be disregarded My plea is for the children and in favour of abandoning a system which is little better than cramming, where the teaching power is very largely under the influence of clerical authority and where social and moral considerations have not the attention they demand in the best interest of the children themselves. To the untrained mind, with an undeveloped conscience and a wayward will, bad influ- ences are always too ready to claim aktheir own, and if you think a few goody-goody sermons with the Cate- chism or any other religious formula is a sufficient safe- guard against the corrupting influences which sur- round the young, you are in my opinion woefully mistaken. Now that the borough is to become a county borough and have the full control of all its affairs, and in view of the establishment of secondary education under the new Act of Parliament, it seems to me a fitting opportunity to raise the question among the managers of our elementary schools, whether the time has not Come for drastic changes in the direction of a more thorough system of primary education which shall be preparatory and fit in with our secondary education and be the medium of securing to all rich and poor alike,the highest and best education within our means. It is a duty we all owe to cho young, the rich to the poor, the strong to the weak, to so equip our youth with the best educational influences that will enable them to fight the battle of life successfully. The CHAIRMAN said he was sure they had all listened to Mr. Monks with great satisfaction. The opinions expressed were such as they could cordially agree with. The Rev. C. H. UOCKEN proposed a vote of thanks to Mr. Monks and referred to the honourable position of Mr. Monks in the town. He (Mr. Hocken) admired the earnestness and sincerity of tone of the address they had just heard. Mr. J.W. HAYWARD seconded the vote of thanks which was carried.

MONKS, in responding, said he had always taken an interest i:4 educational work and he hoped he should live to see the erection of a technical school in Warring ton, provided with the best appliances and thoroughly equipped for its important work. He also hoped ttot some day they would be able to get a school board in the town whereby a better system of elementary educa- tion would be adopted than was possible at present. A vote of thanks having been tendered to the Chair- man, the Benediction was pronounced by the Rev. H. G. Roberto, M.A.

S. Barnabas' Bible Class.

The members of the young men's bible Class in connection with S. Barnabas' Church, had a trip to New Brighton on Saturday afternoon. Accompanied by tlat Vicar (Rev. J El 43 Hollingworth), the party left Central Station at 1.50 arriving at New Brighton at 3.30. The weather was beautifully fine and the pleasure seekers at once dispersed to spend the afternoon aee',3rding to their inclinations. At 4.30 a capital knife and fork tea was provided and ample justice was done to the meal. The party afterwards visited the Tower Grounds and the various amuse- ments were well patronized. The water chute and electric railway were a source of pleasure to many of the excursionists who enjoyed this exhilarating form of recreation. The return journey was com- menced at 830, Warrington being reached at 0,55 after a most successful outing,

THE EXAMINEB, FRIOAY, SEPTEMBER 21, 1900.

A SERVANT GIRL'S SAD END AT STOCKTON HEATH. A Mother Severely Censured.

lAt noon on Tuesday, Mr. Brighouse, coroner, conduc- ted an inquiry in the Parade Room at the Police Station, Warrington, respecting the death of Rebecca Gregory, 17 years of age, domestic servant, in the service of Mr. Walter Jackson. of 9, Grappenhall-road, who died at the Infirmary on Monday morning, from taking a dos ,t el carbolic acid. Mr. Pickavance was foreman of the jury. The Coroner read the police report, which stated that owing to the deceased's ungovernable temper, she received notice to leave the service of Mr Walter Jack- son. About 4.30 on Saturday afternoon, M. Jackson and his wife and children w-mt into a field opposite his house, and whil it there he noticed a boy knocking at the door. As the servant did not attend to him, "Air. Jack- son went 0 the house and found the servant sitting in a chair in a stupefied condition. He at once went for , Dr. Park r,and she was taken home and afterwards to I the Infirmary, where she died. Serge tntßourne, of the Cheshire County Police, examined the house and found a small vessel containing carbolic acid. Dr. Battle, senior house surgeon of the Infirmary, wrote saying that the deceased died from corrosive poisoning, having in all probability taken a dose of carbolic acid. Mary Ellen Gregory, widow, said the decelsed, who was 17 years of are, was her daughter. She had been in the employ of Mr and Mrs Jackson two years and ten months. She saw her on Wednesday last, when she seemed to be "in the best of spirits." She was then on notice to leave. The Coroner asked witness if she had ever thrashed deceased, for, he said, he had heard a rumour to the effect that she had done so, evidently for leaving her place. Witness : I never touched her.

The Coroner : Did you hive word i with your daugh- ter on Wednesday night because she was under notice?

Witness : None whatever.

The Coroner Then if the gentleman has told me ' this morning that " it is common report that you gave your daughter a good hiding," it is untrue ? Witness : Yes, Continuing she said that deceased was of a very excitable dieposicien. See had net heard her threaten to take her own life. Mr. and Mrs. Jackson had been very kind to her. Mr. Walter Jackson, bank clerk, residing at 9, Grappenhall.road, said the deceased had received notice to leave his service many times, but they had taken her on again. Her present notice expired a week ago, but they took her on for a week longer so that she would be able to find a situation. About 420 on Saturday after- nixie last he, his wife and his children went into a field opposite his house and while there he noticed that a boy was knocking at the back door, and seeing that the door was not opened be (witness) went into the house where he found the deceived sitting in a chair in a stupefied condition and no:iced a strong smell of car belie acid in the room. He went for Dr. Parker and she was taken to the Infirmary. Deceased's uncontrollable temper had been the source of great anxiety to his wife. On Saturday she was asked by her mistress why she was not dressed, whereupon she lost her temper and his wife had to leave the house. He had heard that she was not comfortable at home. He was unable to say from whom she got the carbolic acid. None had been in his house for fifteen months.—ln reply to Inspector Phillips witness said that he had never heard the girl threaten to take her life.

Sergeaat Bourne, stationed at Stockton Heath, said that he searched Mr- Jackson's house to try and find the bottle in which she had had the carbolic acid. There was a very strong smell in the scullery and kitchen of the acid. Failing to find any bottle, he went to the local chemist, who said he had sold some carbelic acid to a girl whose description answered that of the deceased. The chemist said she brought a lemonade bottle for it, and said that she wanted it for her mistress. He labelled the hole '• Poison " with two labels. He (witness) afterwards found the small pan produced, which smelt of the acid. He had since made inquiries, and heard that decPased was uncom- fortable at home. He had heard that deceased hid sail that before she would go home she would drown herself. Her mother had not a very good reputation and too k more drink than she ought to do. Mary Elizabeth Coultsou, Chapel-lane, Stockton Heath, said the deceased was her niece- Doceased had been to her house cryicg many times complaining of her mother's bad conduct and she bad threatened to drown herself. Her mother had pawned her clothes for the purpose of buying drink, and had taken deceased's wages off her. She thought that the deceased's bad temper had been caused by the ill-usage she received at the hands of her mother, and that she had been driven to her sad end.

Mrs Grezory was a,ain called, and said : What my sister-in-law has said is not correct. The Coroner : But what about the evidence of Ser- geant Booms? Witness : I know I have taken" a glass or two "but I was driven to it The Foreman: Did you pawn your daughter's clothes? Witness : Yes, when I was short of money.

The Coroner in addressing the jury, said that from the evidence it appeared that the girl hated her home, and that being the case he could quite understand her taking away her life, when she found that she would have to go home. The jiry having considered their verdict, the fore- man said they thought the deceased had committed suicide in a fit of temper and insanity, and asked the Coroner t 3 censure the mother for her conduct. The jury entirely exonerated Mrs. Jackson from any blame. The Coroner said he entirely agreed with the jury. Addressing the mother of the deceased, he said that her daughter went into that state of mind because she did not like the idea of going home. The jury had asked him to cengure her, and it gave him great pleasure to do so, because it had been made clear that she had behaved very badly indeed. He hoped that she would behave better to her other children than she had done to this one. He would not lik3 to be in her position. Ha also warned her against saying anything to her sister-in-law.

'SCHOOL ATTENDANCE OFFICERS' CONFERENCE. Proceedings at Warrington.

Speech by the Mayor.

At the Town Hall, Warrington, on Saturday afternoon , the guar:erly conferenc s of the L tecashire, Cleshire ' and North Wales Federation of the School Attendacce Officer's National Associaton was heid. Councillor Shillcock (vice-chairman of the Corp,ratio.i School Attendance Committee) presided. The otker members of the committee present were Councillors Eilisou and Peacock, accompanied by Mr. T. Birchali. There was a good atterviance of delegates, which include I Mr. Joseph Cooper (president of the National Ass,ciation of , School Attendance Officers). Mr. 0 E Roberts (president I of the Federation), and Mr. Alfred A. Turner (bon secretary and treasurer). The membership of the association numbers an increase of 48 ou last year.

===== 0002601_19000921_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
SPEECH BY THE CHAIRMAN.

The CHAIRMAN apologized for the absence of the Mayor, who, he said, had been compelled to go to Liverpool on business connected with the Corporation. The Chairman of the Warrington Corporation School Attondance Committee (Councillor Shaw Green) was a gentleman who had made school attendance (day and Sunday) the stuiy of a life-time. He regretted to say that he was seriously ill and could not be present, and he (the speaker) had, therefore, to praside. Councillot John Roberts was also unable to be present, owing to absence from town. In the name of the School Attend- ance Committee he (the Chairman) welcomed all the delegates to Warringt ;it was the wish of all the mem- bers of the Committee that they should spend a pleasant afternoon. He contended that ic was only right that meetings should be held, where opinions could be exchanged and steps taken to render school attendance as complete as pessible. He knew the delicate and onerous duties a school attendance officer had to plr- form. His work required the exercise of great tact and intelligence ; while with regard to the committees great discretion was also required,in disposing of the case that came under their notice to decide whether they were dea!ing with people in genuine distress or with people who were trying to deceive them. If these was any method by which they could amend the present system of school attendance it would be a great boon to all of them. He should certainly be pleased t, do all he could to further the interests of the Associati.n. (Applause).

Ag Vice-Chairman of the Museum, Library,Arts, and Technical Instruction Corn- mite, he took some interest in art, and that day there had been a private view of the pictures ha connection win the Autumn Exhibition at Liverpool, and that was toe first time Warrington had been honoured with an invitation to attend. sib'e cheek—(laughter)—of which the Mayor was said to be oceasionally possessed, and the result of a personal application made by him. He did not like such an opportunity to pass unrecogniz -d for the sako of his successors in office. (Hear, hear.) be must apologize for the very shocking condition which the Town Hall buildings were in at the present moment, but those present could tell from the wires that they were in a state of suspense and dirtiness. Such a tuilding as it was, they had pleasure in allowing them to use. (Applause.) He considered the present meetiog an extremely important one, and that the men who were school attendance officers were men whose dutifs were of an important character—duties that were b9ooming increasingly important. The education of the pf-ople of this country in its higher stage must, be well attended to during the next generation or two. and every man who is not fully alive to tie importance of that must be either a fool or a man who went about with his a eyes shut if the commercial prosperity and the continued progress of the Empire were to be maintained it would have to be maintained on different lines in future. And if there was one thing more than another which would make for their future progress it would be the education of the people in the more technical and higher branches than had obtained hitherto. Those who had any acquaintaece with Continental matters knew that it was largely owing to the cultivation of the higher branches of education in Germany and other places that these rivals of ours—we were by no.means beaten yet— had become so formidable during the last generation and they would become increasiuglv so unless we took a lesson from their book. Man for man the Britisher was as good as any man under the sue, and if they gave him as good an equipment there was no' fear about the future in the commercial warfare of the world. (Applause.) The technical instruction they had to give their own peop:e, and the education in higher branches, could not be carried out unless elemen- tary educatien was thorougly well attended to. A good foundation was the work of all superstructure. It did not matter what line they took. Unfortunately, educa- tion for its own sake was not valued by a very large proportion of the working classes of this country. He was glad to think, however, that there were in every town and village all ever the United Kingdom scores of fathers and mothers who had made in dsys gene by and were still making, great sacrifieen for the future progress and prosperity of their children, out that did not obtain to the extent they would like to see. Hence Parliament in its wisdom adopted various educational measures which enabled authorities to appoint officers to look after school attendance. There was a necessity to have the best men who could be obtained. Everything which could be done to conduce to a better stamp of men should be done—men who realizod that they were not doing their duty to the Corporation who paid them their salaries but something for every child they com- pelled to attend school, and through them something to maintain the future prosperity and glory of that magni- ficent Empire. (Applause.) Those were his sentiments, and he should be pleased to do all he could to assist the association in the excellent work in which it was en- gaeed. (Applause.) JONES (Warrington), seconded by Mr. BAMBER, the School Attendaace Committee were thanked for their hospitality.

THE BOATING FATALITY ON THE

MERSEY.

A Warrington Apprentice Drowned.

Mr. Brighouse, county coroner, conducted an in- quiry at tae Dog and Partridge Inn, Poulton-with- Faarnhead,at noon on Monday, respecting the death of Thomas Fernley, a watchmaker's apprentice,who was drowned in the Met.ley whilst boating. On Thursday afternoon last week, the deceased and a companion, named William Henry Appleton, hired a boat at Howley and went up the river for a row. When near Woolst4n, the deceased, who was steer- ing, stood up in the boat and overbalanced himself. He caught hold of the boat, but in doing so over- turned it, and both he and his friend were thrown into the water. Appleton, being able to swim, reached the shore in safety, but the deceased sank. —The foreman of the jury was Mr. Hoult. Frank Fernley, 11, IVhite-street, Warrington, said the the iased was hia brother, and that he was 16 years of age. H was an apprentice watch mak,!r. EL3 81W him hat alive at 830 oa the moriaing, of Thursday Septmnber 13th. Deceased could not swim.

William Henry Appleton, 6, Bridgewater-atret-t, War- rington, an apprentice watchmaker, said that at about three o'cl'cic on Thursday afternoon he and the deceased hired a boat from John Platt, at H3wlev, for an hour, and went up the river, in the direction of Thelwalt. Deceased steered with an oar and he rowed. When opposite the Bone Works, Wootaton, he asked the deceased what kind of works they were, and the deceased said he thought they were sand works. Just then he stood up, and also lifted his oar up. The boat rocked and he overbalanced himself. He caught hold of the back of the seat, but it being loose, he fell into the water. The boat turned upside down, and he (witness) was also thrown into the water. His foot was fast in the boat and it was with great diffioul:y that he swam to the shore, where he undressed and dived in for deceased but before he could get to him he had sunk. In reply to the Coroner, he said the boat was licenced for four per- sons. He could not say whether it was an out rigged or an in rigged boat._

The Coroner said that the youths could not know much about boats. It was a very dangerous thing to stand in a small boat.

John Platt said he was a joiner, living in Howley- lane. He kept a boat house and let boaca out on hire. About three o'clock the deceased and Appleton hired a boat for an hour, and on their not returning he took a boat, about six o'clock, and went in search of them. He was informed that Fernley had been drowned, and on the following day he dragged the river and recovered the body at nine o'clock that day, and took it to the Dog and Partridge. It was an outrigged boat, was 22ft long, 4ft 6m beam, and was licenced to hold four. He gave them two pairs of oars and they refused to have a rudder.

The Coroner :—lt is suprising how many people think they know how to manage a boat when they really d n't, They think they could jump about in it as if they were on a platform on land. The Foreman :—How long have you k'spt boats ? Witness :—ren years, and thislii the second drowning Case I have had.

The Coroner :—Considering how ignorant people are I 91-ould think that i a good record. The jury returned a verdict of " Accidental Death."

LOCAL HARVEST FESTIVALS.

Wycliffe Congregational Church.

Harveat thanksgiving services were held morning and evening in Wycliffe Congregational Church, Warrington. on Sunday, and there were fairly large congregations at both services. No elaborate decorations had been car- ried out, but a ceoice colectien of lilies, dahlias, p'ants and ferns was displayed in front of the pulpit. The Rev. J. Yonge was the preacher and based his morning discourse on the 50-11 Psalm, verse 14 :—" Offer unto G-id thanksgiving and pay Thy vows unto the Most High." lie said surely it was meet that such season should arrest thought and touch the heart. It bore witness of man's dependence upon God and his con- sequent obligation to God. As a rule we were very slow to recognize and realize how manifold and continual the Divine mercies were. The preacher proceeded to point out that our thanksgiving should be entirely voluntary and that according to the nature of the gift it should be without blemish. A special anthem entitled "Ye shall dwell" (Stainer) was sung,the solo parts being taken by Miss E Knowles and Mr. J Knowlea. At the evening service, the Rev. J Yonge based his sermon on the text. " When thou bast eaten and art full, then thou shalt bless the Lord thy God for the good land which be bath given thee. Beware that thou forget not the Lord thy God, in not keeping His corn andmenta, and Hi 3 judg- mentAtand His statutes.which I command thee this day." He stated that one great and good purpose at any rate was served by a festival of praise and thanksgiving in the season of harvest for it served ta give conspicuous- ness and emphasis to the fact that God was Lord of the harvest. After dilating upon the reason for thankfulness to God he stated that men who forgot God were a god unto themselves. Rom, times it seemed as though this forgetfulness of God was coming to be a most promi- nent characteristic of the ago in which they lived. The rapidly-spreading secularisation of Sunday was a featuae of it. The anthem " Father of Mercies " (J. E. West), was creditably rendered by the choir, Mrs. Pear- son and Mr. A. Griffiths singing the solo parts. The anthem "Sing unto the Lord " (BarnbN) was also sung, Mr. Payton presided at the organ with his usual efficiency. The collections for the day amounted to £25.
