ered, however, that there was a transition being made to quick-firing guns about this time, and that a pause was necessary until experi- ments had been completed. We come to artillery. First as to Field and Horse Artillery. Mr. STAN- HOPE, Sir H. CAMPBELL-BANNERAIAN'S immediate predecessor, expended £133,000 on this head, but reduced the number of batteries. Hence in the Years 1893-4 only £79,000 was expended on this head. Probably the change to quick-firers had not theu taken place, and when it was made new guns and gun-carriages were ordered; and hence the increase of outlay from June. 1895, and there- after. The expenditure on heavy artillery tells the same story. Mr. STANHOPE spent more in 1890-2 than any of his successors did. His provi- sion cost £962,000. Sir H. CAMPBELL-BANNERMAN spent £254,000, and in the first two years Lord LANSDOWNE only thought fit to lay out £315,0001 ruder this head, with £592,000 in 1897-9. These variations were not due to differences of policy, but to other matters. When large expenditure on particular items occur in two successive years, it will naturally fall. Neither artillery nor ammu- nition disappears when put into stock. The charge of deficiency is thus unfair. Mr. STANHOPE expended on warlike stores the sum of £2,669,000 in the two years before the Liberal Government of 1892 came into office. In two years the Liberal Government expendod £1,28e,000 on the same items. Mr. BRODRICK calls this " unduly stinting the supply of military stores." Well, LORD LANSDOWNE came in, and in the succeeding .two years he only expended £1,747,000, or an increase of £482,000, including cordite orders, quick-firing guns, field artillery, and big gun ammunition. The dispute is thus by no means a serious one. There was no great change of policy, but experiments had finished, and projects not originated by LORD LANSDOWNE were carried out by him. The public feels little interest in these technical disputes. If they shew anything at all they show that expendi- ture is always-fluctuating, and that the permanent or non-political advisers of the War Office are not always of the same mind. The enormous exertions made up to March 1899, to prepare for war in South Africa did not result in an expenditure on Warlike stores equ tl to that made in 1890-2, when no war threatened, by a sum of £159,000, which ahews that a Unionist Government was actually providing fewer stores a few months before war broke out that it was in 1890-2. Mr. BRODRICK'S accusation is thus a reflection upon his own friends as much as on his political opponents.

THE old question as to whather a Chureh of England clergyman owns the freehold of his church has once more been raised in the Revision Courts. It is well known that there are clergy- men who claim property in the church of which they are incumbents, and talk of "my church" just in the same way as they talk of the National schools being "my schools." This view is not sus- tained by the law of the land, so far as legal decis- ions on the point are intelligible. At the Revision Court for mid-Northamptonshire two clergymen claimed the franchise on the ground that they Were freeholders in the parish church; and two others, already on the list of freeholders were ' objected to on the ground that they had nu freehold in the church. The case of the Rev Thomas Hands, vicar of St Lawrence', Northampton, was taken as a test case, the others being similar. It was claimed that he had a "forty-shilling interest arising directly out of land," being entitled to receive fees in respect Of marriages, bmptisms and churchings which took Place in the church, whether he!personally officiated thereat or not, the fees being for the use of the church. It was replied that these fe,?s were for Personal service; he could get another clergyman to discharge the service for him, but that did not ' affect the question. This was the view taken by Mr W H Disney, the revising barrister. He held that a clergyman had not power to let oft his church, and that fees were not rent ; and therefore he disallowed the two claims,and upheld the objec- tion in the third case. In the fourth, however, it was shown that the herbage in the churchyard was let for £3 a year, the proceeds going to the clergy- man, and the right to vote as a freeholder was therefore allowed. Hence the parson of the parish has a freehold in the churchyard, but not in the church. Revision Court law almost beats " Crowner's Quest law" in its vagaries; but, after all, ecclesiastical law is, pehaps, worst of all to interpret,as brighter legal luminaries than revising barristers know well.

It is satisfactory to know that the feeling of irritation, if not of positive hostility, against England and all things English which had obtained somehow possession of the Parisian mind, is rapidly passing away. One of the reasons is, doubt- less, the wave of prosperity which has swept over the Exhibition, though too late, it is feared, to make up in the end the comparative failure of the first few months. Even the splendid weather of the last few weeks has done something to smooth matters, for Paris, after all, prefers her gay round of pleasures to the acerbities of international strife. There had been a general idea that the non-success of the great Exhibition was due to an English boycott, and it is, no doubt, a fact that some Classes in Paris suffered injury from the abstention of English visitors. This attitude, however, is now at an end ; the English people know that the Nationalist papers whose excesses caused indignation in this country, represent but a few rabid politicians, while on the other hand, Prenchmen are better able to recognise where the blur e for the little misunderstanding is really to be laid. Trio incident, indeed, is now almost for- gotten. English people may walk along the boule- vardes with as little risk of insult as the French- man experienos in the Strand ; and, as a matter Of fact, there are at the present a large number of Englishmen in Paris, though in the total of strangers they may be outnumbered by Americans, by Russians, and even by Germans. And the last risk of misunderstanding appears lately to have been removed by the fiasco which has attended the attempt to make of the municipal festivities a great Nationalist demonstration.

Our Conservative friends in Warrington have Practised what might be termed the doctrine of convenient foNetfillness until, with them, it has become almost a fine art. Last week our .political contemporary made capital out of the fact that the Chancelior of the Duchy in appointing seven new magistrates had conferred the honour on two Radicals ani two Liberal Unionists in ad- diti Tri to three Conservatives. This, we are invited to look upon as an example of Con- servative ulagnaiiinaity which could not be bettered, attention being also drawn, in contrast, to the elevation to the Bench in 1892 of Liberal j.P.'s only. By the exercise of this convenient forgetfulness our contemporary has arrived at a -conclusion which must have been gratifying, but which, unfortunately, will not " hold water" The Conservative enagnanimity—a doubtful quality at the hest—is considerably discounted by yearsprior to 1892, which the history of a few Year is so itegenuously made the basis of compari- lion. In the years 1886, 1888, and 1889 the Con- kiervative Chancellor of the Duchy created no less then twelve magistrates, and all of them Conserva- tives. Prior to 1886, we believe, whenever any Liberal oppointments to the magisterial bench were made there was always one, or more, mem- bers ol the oppomite-,.party similarly and " mag- naninion-Oy " olstingnished. It was not until 1892, and only then, that the Liberals took th- opportunity of remedying to some extent the disadvantages under which they lahourel as a result of the unfair and unjusti- fiable partizanship of their opponents in the past. truer estimate of the present Conservative policy mild he to style it not "magnanimity," but Making amends."

At the present time we The Shabbiness of" Party." are supposed to be in crisis. The Imperial position of this country is declared to be at stake. The patriotism of the electors is appealed to. They are to remember what is due to the country and her colonies, and our general interests abroad' They are to be high-minded, noble, magnani- mous Imperialists. What they settle is to be something in the nature of a new basis for the British Empire, a code for its statesmen, a principle of action for its politicians. They are to rise above party into some more exalted atmosphere. Yet the time and conditions of the appeal are selected to obtain party advan- tage in the shabbiest possible manner. There has been no time for careful and elaborate preparation for the great national verdict. For months past Ministers have treated a dissolution as if it were a joke. They pro- fessed not to know when it was coming. TheY denied that a time had been fixed. But It was settled in principle long ago, and the secret of the date was carefully hidden because it was thought to be disadvantageous to opponents that the time should not be known Lord Salisbury returns from a Continents/ trip, and, hey presto! the dissolution is fixed up and declared in a few days. The whole thing was a shabby party trick, perpetrated by the new Imperialists, not in tha interests of Cr EMPIRE, but in the interests of LORD SALISBURY s

Ministry. An American Boss could not have done the thing more cunningly. Many member° arewho intend to again contest constituencies absent. Some are fighting for the Burl" ie. .a South Africa. A dashing surprise is effected, an then it is trumpeted abroad as a sign of Oppositlon. weakness that many seats will be uncontested- ' Never in our political history has there been 81111 a shabby party trick. The occasion so the Afill.ha terialists say, is solemn and significant. nut appeal is fixed at a time when the Parliarnenter' register is old, when electors put on the register , whilst the war in South Africa has been proceed" ing cannot vote, and when there will be as wall electors disfranchised as there were Outlanders al the Transvaal, in whose political enfranchiseineo Mr. CHAMBERLAIN attempted such futile negetle" tions. The Ministry knew what the effect would be. Mr. BALFOUR was asked to pass a measure to expedite the registration this year, so as to nie'4 the possibility of an autumn dissolution. He de- liberately refused to do anything of the kind. Ile preferred the old registers with their disfranchise - meats. When it was a question ot giving counti householders as such, a chance to vote oil domestic questions, there was a new register' When the Empire is supposed to be at stake, deliberate preference is given to an old register' A surprise dissolution on such a great question la bad enough, but to take a national verdict on tal obsolete and maimed register is the lowest partY trick that politicians can play. There is n° urgency. The Empire will not be in more peril ia January than in October. Great stress is laid on Liberal weakness. But advantage is taken of it io the party interests of the other side. The Govern- ment has a majority of one hundred and thirty', and the Constitution permits it to have another session when it could have settled South Afriew and appealed on a completed work. In rrofessiag• to have regard to the Empire, it shabbily seeks t° damage Liberalism, and ensure its own retention of office for years to come. This is ordinarh vulgar party-spirit, and hotlofty-minded Imperial" ism. The critics of its own side, Sir Enwsan CLARKE, Mr. COURTNEY, and Commander BETHELL, have been hustled, the first already cot of political life, and the others are to be treated al the same way. Mr. BURDETT-COUTTS, who dire' ted attention to the hospitals in South Africa, hes been treated as if he were a renegade and a traitor. This conduct shows to the country what a sham the current Imperialism is.

MATTER OF 'MOMENT. ---- A Provincial Letter.

The Queen has often of late displayed her tact of j doing the right thing at the right time, and in the j most pleasant and unassuming way. Her Niaiesty j has been pleased to assent to the visit of the Duke and Duchess of York to the Colonies of Australasia in the spring of next year, whenuthe Duke will I open the first session ot the Parliament of the Aus- tralian Commonwealth in her name. This is the j latest of her more conspicuous public acts. Only a I day or two before, the War Office told us that a certain number of officers and men, representing ai far as possible, each of the Colonial corps now serv- ing under Lori Roberts, will be invited to visit this country as soon as it becomes posiible to materially reduce the strength of the South African fieldlorce. Her Majesty has further intimated that she will be pleased to inspect the Colonial troops, and to present them with colours comme- morative of the distinguished part they ;have borne in the campaign. The troops will afterwards be lionised in a proper and befitting manner. first in London, and then in the principal cities and towns of the provinces.

Notwithstanding the innumerable "tips " that had been given by more or less reliable personages and" authorities," many constituencies were taken by surprise on Tuesday evening when the news was flashed from Balmoral that the Dissolution of Par- liament would take place on September 25th. In the fortnight or so before the election commences however, many things may happen, and already Divisions that were supplied with candidates have suddenly become very active. So, alsp, are the prophets. Since it is to be a khaki election, the seers have made cocksure of the return of the same party to power, with an overwhelming majority. But some of the more modest, or moderate of the Unionists do not desire to be overwhelmed. They believe,' in the principle envolved in government by party, with each party fairly represented. But what about Disraeles saying, before he became an Earl, that it is the unexpected that generally happens ?

It will at least be a great relief to many parents and others who have had relatives and friends at the front, to be assured that the so-called hospital scandals were much exaggerated. The official inquiry drags wearily on, and it is simply charitable to the well-meaning Mr Burdett-Coutts to say that the evidence only bears out the interpretation here placed upon it. When it is remembered how many families all over the country are affected by the tales that have been told, it becomes important to know only the real truth. Lord Kitchener, the latest important witness before the commission declares that he saw Mr Burdett-Coutts frequently but the latter made not the slightest complaint of the manner in which things:were done. Although a great deal of stir has been made about the matter, the appearances,so far as the inquiry has proceeded and has shown, point to something approaching a complete vindication of the authorities. At any rate the present proceedings have only tended to justify the suggestions offered when the allegations were first published, not to accept unchallenged the shocking story told by Mr Burdett-Coutts.

If Lord Leigh's resolution, adopted by the War- wick Guardians on Saturday, finds favour, the day may come when children will be strangers to the workhouse, at any rate during the most impression- able period of their lives. There is no reason what- ever why bpys and girls should have to start life with the dispiriting taint of "the house" upon them, when such can be avoided without any in- crease and expense to the ratepayers. Things have changed vastly since the days of Oliver Twist for the juvenile pauper. Indeed, the orphans, accord- ing to present day regulations, get the better of it, since the guardians are able to board them out, Lord Leigh's desire is to see the system extended to all children over the age of ten, and there will probably be few objections to the course. But there are to points to be insisted upon. The parents must not be allowed to forget that a responsibility still rests upon them, and that it will be necessary for the guardians to keep a strict supervision over the foster parents whom they find it necessary to employ. In some parts of the provinces ratepayers have complained through the local papers of the cost of these homes, but nothing is heard against the general principle involved.

The enormous, and to many people strange and unjustifiable, increase of the prices of coal has had the effect in the Midlands of bringing together the leading manufacturers, at which gathering it was agreed to make a joint protest. It may not have much effect upon coal-owners ; that is more than could be expected. But a strong representation should at least have the effect of somewhat modify- ing their outrageous demands, and of letting them know what the manufacturers think of their methods. The truth is sometimes good for us to hear, if unpleasant.

Another phase of the coal question is introduced by a correspondent who asserts that previous to the buoyancy of trade the colliery owners allowed 21 cwt. to one ton on truck loads, but the " over- measure" has been reduced to two cwt. per truck discount, for cash payment has been abolished, and threepence per ton added to the track hire. If these statements are true, they only serve to emphasise the fact that colliery pro- prieters,not content with large profits, are actually usurious in their demands. The pity is that cus- tomers are practically helpless.

The Church of England Temperance Society hope to open at Michaelmas a home for the inebriate men who,though not coming under the Act of 1899, are yet inebriates, and are willing voluntarily to seclude themselves for the purpose of overcoming their failing, at such rates of payment as are within their reach. A lady has offered the use of a house and grounds at liancox, near Battle, free of rent for seven years, for the purpose of establishing such a home; and the society appeal for £l,OOO to £1,200 to place the home in working order.

At the conclusion of the Blackburn Licensing Session, the Mayor announced that the magistrates intended, before the next licensing ses4on, to make an inspection of the congested districts in the borough with a view to "regulating" the number of the bouQes to the reasonable requirements of the various districts. They will also take into consi- deration the necessity of imposing heavier fines in proved cases of permitting drunkenness on licensed premises. If other licensing magistrates, in other districts and boroughs would do likewise they would be citing real and prac•ical good.

From wireless telegraphy to wireless telephony is a bigger step than it appears. But in these days science conquers all; so that the world may not have been greatly surpriged to learn that the trans- mission of sounds and of human speech through space without the aid of the familiar wire bas now become a practical and a "commercial system." But the great difficulty, yet to be removed, is, the facility with which messages may be intercepted by anybody with a pair of conductors. Until the difficulty is overcome, wireless telephony will not be extensively used.

As winter approaches the lighting authorities in many provincial towns and urban districts are seen getting their lamps in order, or, like the prudent virgins," trimming them "in readiness for the dark nights that are already setting in. It will be some time yet before many of our towns are electrically lighted, and meanwhile they will have to make the best Use of the gas supplied The experience of those lighting authorities who have fairly and properly tried it, is, that there is nothing as yet to beat incandescent gas lighting. The words" fairly and properly "are used advisedly. For the lanterns of an antiquated fashion will not do; the lamps should be of the required pattern, and the results will justify any little added expanse. For steadi- ness and equal distribution of light, incandescent gas lighting is far superior to electricity; and it has also the recommendation of being much cheaper, But there is an idea abroad that electricity is more up-to-date and " respectable," and that,as it is often supplied by the Corporation, while gas is not, the price does not matter a great deal.

CADBURY'S Cocoa, on the testimony of the Lancet "represents the standard of highest purity." It is entirely free from all foreign substances, such as kola, malt, hops, Ate., nor Is alkali used to darken the colour (and so deceive the eye). Dr. Andrew Wilson, in a recent article in the Illustrated London News writes : "Cocoa is in itself a perfect food, and requires no addition of drugs whatever." CAD- BURY'S Cocoa is absolutely pure, and should be taken by old and yonng, at times and in all seasons; for Children is it an ideal beverage, promoting healthy growth and development in a remarkable degree. A collision occurred alongteide Fleetwood Quay on Thursday last week. As the steamer Cecil. of Runcorn, was coming to moor she collided with the steam coaster City of Liverpool, which was berthed along the quayside. A hole was rent in the City of Liverpool below the water line, caused by the anchor point, and she sank at the quay side. It is expected the vesael will be floated and repaired forthwith. It is with regret we announce the death of Albert Varley of Widnes, aged 21 years, who was for six years the esteemed side drummer in the Nelson-street Wesleyan Sunday School drum and fife band. Deceased's mortal remains were interred at Sankey Church burial ground, on Friday, Sep- tember 14th. The funeral procession left the resi- dence deceased's of the parents in the follow- ing order. Clays. The Nelson-street School committee were representPd by Mr. J. W. Bazley, Superintendent. An artificial wreath was sent by the members of the band as a last token of respect to their deceased comrade.

A WOMAN PRONOUNCES HER OWN SENTENCE AT RUNCORN.

Before the Runcorn magistrates, Messrs. W Timmins and W Handley, on Friday moraing,Mary Jane Walton, a married woman, was charged with stealing a silver watch and chain and a medal, on the 18th of June last, the property of John Molyneaux. Mrs. Phcebe Molyneaux, wife of John Molyneaux,said her husband was a bricksetter. and they resided at 3, New-street, Runcorn. On the 18th of June last, about 20 minutes past eleven, her husband's silver watch and chain with small medal attached were hanging up over the mantelpiece in the kitchen. She was sure it was there at that time, for a neighbour had asked the " time." Witness bad occasion to leave the house a few minutes afterws!ds and when she came back she missed the watch. That morning, the prisoner, who lived four houses up the street, had called upon her and they had gone shepping together. When they returne I she told Mrs Walton about the loss of the watch. Mrs Walton seemed very much concerned about it, and "busied herself about it." She went with witness to the pawnshops, and to the p Mee station giving information about it. She also said" it was a great shame, and whoever had done it deserved 12 months." On Sunday,the 911 day of Sept., witness saw Wilfred Brown, a nephew of the prisoner's, and noticed he had a medal on his chain. She told Mrs Walton about the medal saying it was very like her hus- band's. She replied, " I wonder where he got it from." Witness then expressed a desire to see it,but Mrs Walton did not give her an oppartunity ; and, ultimately said "he (Brown) got it from Wrexham about four years ago." Witness identified the watch chain and medal (produced) as her husband's property. Wilfred Brown, fitter and moulder, Rhos Diu, Wrex- ham, the nephew referred to, swore to visiting his aunt's on the 9h of September. She had given him the medal on a previous occasion, but be did not know how she bad come by it. On that Sunday. however, she had drawn him on one side, and told him "It that women down the street comes and asks where you Rot the medal from, say Wrntham." On Tuesday last, he received a visit from Sergeant Gregory, and handed him the medal as the result of a conversation with him.

Charles Morgan. pawnbroker, 161, Pembroke-place, Liverpool , swore that on Monday, the 2gth of June, the watch (produced) was pledged with him for 71 6d, by the prisoner, in her own nume. Detective Sergeant Gregory said he received informa- tion of the theft, and assisted by his Superintendent, made numerous inquiries which led to the apprehension of the prisoner. He chareed her with the offence, and at first she denied it. Subsequently, she admitted that she had stolen the articles, and told him he would find the watch in " the second pawnshop in London-road, Liverpool." She bad destroyed the ticket. Ile recovered the medal from Brown.

In answer to the charge, prisoner elected to be sum- marily dealt with. The Superintendent of Police said the woman was married and in a good position. He believed she got 32s or 353 per week on which to keep house, and had only one child. It was not an ordinary case or one of sudden temptation, as he knew, of his own knowledge, she had had ample opportunity Of returning it. Be wished, also to state that the county had been put to considerable expense to recover the property, though the theft was but a small one.

Mr. Handley: You know Mrs. Walton, you have pronounced your own sentence ?—At this the prisoner completely broke down. The Chairman, addressing her, said :—Mrs. Walton this is a very serious case, and it is very sad to see you in this position: Considering your condition, and the delicate state of your health, we are, however, disposed to take a very lenient view of it. You will be bound over tor 6 months, and will have to pay £1 14s costs, or 14 days. We hope it will be a warning to you, and it is only your state of health that makes us take this merciful view of it.—The fine was paid. The Superintendent wished the Bench to Exonerate the witnes:s Brown, for he had acted quite candidly with the police in the matter: The Chairman expressed his satisfaction, saying they considered the witness Brown had teen straightforward.

RUNCORN NOI ES.

[By a Correspondent.

The topic which takes the palm for speculative' nese this week is that of the General Election. So far, the Conservatives of the Nortliwich Division are without a candidate, but as several of the lead- ing Tories of the district assert they are going to contest the Division, Liberals generally are employed in a keen competition of guessing "who 'twill be." What rumours are afloat are voiced in another column. With regard to Runcorn's part in the General Election, and it plays a not unimport- ant one, the vast majority of the tomnspeople still favour the "old love," and come what will or who will, Sir John Brunner will fin I the town a capable prop.”

A society of young men who year in and year out accomplish a lot of gratituous work; and a society of young men who deserve greater support than has hitherto been extemded to them, is that of the Runcorn Parish Church Bell-ringers. Few Church people seem to recognise that the ringing of church bells is fast becoming a lost art, and but for the strenuous efforts of a few so far at any rate a 3 Run- corn is concerned the art would indeed be gone without recall. At present the " Beliringers ' are engaged in remounting and decorating their" bel- fry," doing it too "upon their own." So far they have met with a measure of success which ingenu- ity and perseverance alone could command, but there is yet time for the charitably disposed to come forward and assist a deserving hard-worked society.

! That the vaccination question is not dead by any means was simply illustrated at the Runcorn Petty 'Sessions last Monday. when an applicant came for- ward with that terrible scourge to society " a con- scientious objection."• Unlike the Frodsham Bench, the Runcorn magistrates believed the applicant when he said he had "got it," and sent him on his way rejoicing with the 'exemption. Now I am not opening a discussion on the "vaccination question,' and any correspondence sent to me on the question will immediately go to the" w-p-b," but I do wish to t-uly this:—When a " rash "or anything ails a child, distracted parents, rush off at once to the Doctor's, and trust the child entirely to him; yet, when once the same Doctor hints at vaccination as a preventative of that "rash or ailment," what do these parents do? They simply tura round pre- pared to argue the point. Truly, there is a strange inconsistency in our nature. Mr.liandley's remarks at court were of a sensible nature and should set sensible people thinking.

This winter when Runcorn tradespeople and householders are contemplating the size of their gas bills, they might also consider the advisability of purchasing Runcorn Gas Works. Of course, I know the first question will be can the town afford it? Indeed, that questiun always does crop up But it would be far better if those people who most often raise it, would try first to answer it them- selves. Rather they should ask "Can the town afford to go cn paying the high price it does at pre- sent for its gas ?" Why, the Gas Works is almost being !bought every decade ! Can the suicidal policy be allowed to continue? Tnat is rather the question ! Personally, I should like to see the Chairman of Finance (Mr Imison) take the matter in hand. Ha could summon his colleagues to- gether, and see what could be done: for, that the town should control its own light is beyond all argument ; and, if the matter was taken up, and not lost sight of, the end of five years should see this step in the right direction consummated. Furthermore. Mr Imison has the confidence of the town to back him up, and could he be prevailed upon to act with the present Chairman of the Coun- cil (Mr Handley) we would at any rate get to know how the land lay.

FOOTBALL.

Runcorn will visit St. Helens on Saturday, and will be represented by last week's team with the exteption of two. Jolley, who, owing to injuries received against Broughton, will stand down for Richardson, and C Butterworth, will be replaced by Wilkinson. St. Helens, too, will be a man or two short of their full strength as fears are entertained that Doherty their resourceful three-quarter will be unable to figure in the team. As both teams are beginning to get into the stride:a goad game is an- ticipated. The corresponling match was won by St. Helens, when it will be remembered Houghton made that "fatal fall" and Briers got in with a try, and just snatched victory for the Ssints' by a try to a dropped goal (3 points to 2 points). The Runcorn ream will be as follows :—Houghton, back ; Wilkinson. J Butterworth, T Warder, and D Jones, three- quarters; Faulkner and Richardson. halves; Walker. Lightfoot, Farmer, Gayter, Dixon, Messen- ger, and Reece. and Tomlinson. forwards; Williams, reserve.

Runcorn's injured players are in a fair way to re- covery, and are progressing as well as can be ex- pected. Jimmy Langley has been to Dr. Jones, and it has been ascertained that he is suffering from an impaired circulation of the arm and overstrainei muscles, lie is recommended for a course of massage, and will be attend to by Doctor Stubbs (the popular trainer) who no doubt will bring him up to the scratch very soon. Jolley has the mis- fortune to have two bones fractured, at the elbow and shoulder He, Loa, has been attended by Dr. Jones, Liverpool, and owing to his fine constitution is likely to make a rapid recovery. Jolley is a re- markable fellow. and I dare say has sustained as many bone fractures than any living footballer. I trust for the future he may be lucky enough to play the game with safety.

THE GENERAL ELECTIONS

Northwich Division.

The One Bright Spot in Cheshire.

At the last General Election the Northwich Divi- sion from the Liberal point of view was the one bright spot in Cheshire. Sir John T. Brunner,Bart., its representative, was the only Liberal member in Cheshire, until joined some years later by an old opponent, Mr. George Whiteley, of Stockport, who renounced the heresies of the Tory party and took a seat on the Liberal benches. Sir John Brainier, with the exception of one brief twelve months, has represented Northwich Division since 1885, when the last redistribution of seats took place ; and at each election the majority in his favour has very materially increased. At the 1895 elec- i ion his opponent was Mr. Thomas Ward, a director of the Salt Union, a man of considerable local in- fluence, and Sir John defeated him by the over- whelming majority of 1,638. The gentleman,there- fore, who aspires to oust the worthy baronet will require to have some courage and at the time of writing the Tory party have brought forward no candidate. About two years ago the Conservatives and Liberal Unionists determined to contest the seat and a selection committee was appointed but the only apparent result of this committee's work was a plentitul crop of rumours. Amongst the names mentioned were those of Mr A Hornby Lewis, of Marbury Hall, who refused to stand for Oldham, and Major Mosley Leigh, now in South Africs, in command of the 22nd Company of Imperial Yeomanry; but neither of the gentle- men, it is stated, will be forthcoming. This week Dame Rumour has been busy with the name of a younger scion of the House of Egerton, and there ' appears to be no doubt that the Tory wire- pullers have cast their eyes in that direction, but no definite announcement hPs been made. This (Friday) afternoon a meeting of the leaders of the Conservative and Unionist parties is to be held at Ncrthwich and the mystery will be cleared up. Prominent Conservatives, how- ever, express the utmost confidence that Sir John will not be allowed a wdlk over. At present Sir John is on the Continent, but may be expected home on any day. The population of the North- wich Division is 69,893 ana the electorate 11,992. Hundreds will be disfranchised through ths electlon being fought on an old register as the division lies a large working-class population. At this time the utterances of members of oppo- site political creeds, are, like the straws in the street, indications of "how the wind blows." There- fore, to put into print the expression :of one of Runcorn's most Conservative Conservatives, regard- ing the question of contesting the Division will not be out of place. It is a candid admission, too, of the worth of Sir J T Brunner. He says: " What is the use of opposing the genial baronet? It would be useless ; for, it is doubtful if the division would get as good a representative. Sir John is always doing good; ever ready to subscribe to this or that fund ; to ventilate the suffrages of all ; and is never doing any harm. What more do we want ?" This is truly a blunt admission of St. John's excel- lence, and we believe too that it is but an expres- sion of cordiality towards Sir John that is felt by very many Conservatives throughout the Division Warrington. - -

It is probable that there Will ba a contes`, in War- rington, but as the prospective Liberal candidate, Mr. A II Crosfield has not yet reached this country from America nothing is known definitely. There is to oe a meeting of the Liberal executive this (Friday) evening, when the position will be dis- cussed, and Mr. Crosfield probably adopted. Mr R Pierpoint, M.P., the present member reached the town yesterday, and addressed a meeting of the Conservative Council last evening,

Newton Division.

The official announcements ac to the early dissolu- tion of Parliament came somewhat as a surprise to the electors of the Newton Division resident in the Earlestown and Newton portion, although the officials on either political side appeared to be quite prepared to take immediate action whenever required. At the Conservative headquarters there was plenty ot bustle, and Mr Rolland-Owen the Conservative agent was early on the:scene at the offices of the Conservative Association, Newton The probability of a contest, however, hi the New- ton Division is very remote. Although attempts have been made, more particularly during the last year, to organise the Liberal party, the absence, to use Parliamentary language, of a prolonged nursing of the constituency has • allowed the Conservative party to secure a strong hold upon the division. In Colonel R. Pilkington the Conservatives feel they have a very strong man, and generally he is held in much respect, personally and politically speaking. The organising Liberal agent, Mr Hl3 Shores, is at present occupied in the revision court at Widnes, and,seeing that the Liberals are contest- ing Widnes again this election, it is impossible that he will be able to devote his energies to both divisions,. Rumours have been current that the Labour party might oppose the election of Colonel Pilkingten. The working class are predominant in the central portion of the constituency, and no doubt a candidate would poll a large number of votes; but it is very improbable that a candidate will be nominated, from the act that both the Miners' and Engineers' Federations would find it very difficult to support the candidate if successful. . It is stated that Mr Sam Woods was in Newton last week end conferring with a prominent member of the local branch of the I.L.P. but to what end we are at present unable to say.

FIRES AT WIDNES.

Serious Damage at a Farm.

Late on Friday evening, a barn containing the crop of wheat from twelve acres of land at Rock Farm, Upton, Widnes, and occupied by Mr. Miles Verity, was discovered to be on fire. An alarm was raised and steps were taken to prevent the fire spreading to the adjoining stables and shippons', in which were five horses and twelve head of cattle, as well as agricultural implements, &e. The efforts in this line were successful. The Widnes Fire Brigade, under Foreman Beezley, responded smartly to the summons, and were soon on the scene with their steam fire engine. At four o'clock on Satur- day morning the fire was mastered. It was then found that the whole of the produce, which was fortunately insured, was destroyed and the shippon and stables had been much damaged. The total amount of damage to the crops was £2OO. ' The buildings, which belong to Mr. Thomas Lawson, Dingle Brook Farm, West Kirby, Liverpool, were uninsured. The fire brigade did not leave the fire until noon on Saturday. Blaze at Bolton's Copper Works. Fire brAre out at Messrs. Bolton and Sons' Copper Works, early on Saturday morning. A furnace con- taining about 26 tons of molten copper burst, and the fiery I,quid quickly spread over the floor of the building, which was soon in fi imes. The heat was so intense that the rafters on the rocf became ignited. Informa- tion was sent to the fire station, and a portion of the brigade turned out with the manual and reel cart. Thanks to the exertions of the brigade, together with Sergeant Carter and several constables, the fire was soon extinguished. The damage, which we under- stand is covered by insurance, is estimated at £5O or £6O.

Alexandra Theatre in Flames,—Panic Stricken Audience.

_ On Monday night a fire occarrei:at the Alexandra Theatre, owned by Messrs. Garrard, Bray and Kiddie. Messrs. Conquest and LToaara's company were giving a perfcrmance of the piss, "Serving the Queen." and the building was packed with people. About 920 several ladies and gentlemen in the front row noticed sparks dropping from the roof of the stage. One of the gentle- , men called the attention of the orchestra leader, who in turn acquainted the actors. The latter immediately dis- covered chat the scenery had become ignited by one of the gas jets in the rear of the bui'ding. What was to be most feared next occurred. Toe audience became , aware of the serious danger which surrounded them and, rising en masse, rnshed for the doors. The con- srquences might have been terrible had not several of the company, as well as a numb-r of local gentlemen, mounted the stage and assured the crowd that there was no immeaiate danger. The feelings of the people.which had become worked up to a pitch almost amounting to frenzy, were calmed, and thanks to the gallant efforts of the gentlemen mentioned, the auclieuce left the build- ing as quietly as could be expected, what would un- doubteely have proved a calamity being happily , averted. In the meantime an eft ort had been made to drop the iron curtain, but this refused to move, which was probably due to the heat having caused expansion. Toe stage hosepipm was requisitioned, and at once at- tached to the hydrant, and the actors put forth their best endeavours to cope with Ile flames. These, how- ever, proved of no avail, as the fire quickly spread to the roof and the other portion of the scenery. Informa- tion was despatched to the Fire SAtion, and in a very brief space of time the brigade, under Fireman Gerrard, were upon the scene with the'r steam engine. Vslumes of water were thrown on to the building, which still continued to bale with unabated fary. Seeeral members of the bric,,adr I on the stage near the side door directing the h csepipe when a portion of the roof collapsed with a thunderous crash, and the men experienced a very narrow eseape, The entire belongings of the company in addition to valuable stage scenery, were utterly destroyed either by fire or water. The actors and actresses had not time to divest themselves of their stage " get-up," and the former presented a beiraggled/appearance as they,rushed about helping wherever they could It was past mid- night before the smouldering fire was completely extinguished. The manager of the company subse- quently stated, in an interview, that the fire originated from the bunch lights. He believed that if the people had only kept calm and maintained their seats, the out- break would have been quelled before it had assumed such serious proportions. Much sympathy is expressed with the proprietors in their misfortune.

On Tuesday, Mr. James Kiddie, man interview, Raid the theatre is in no way seriously damaged. The management had wisely taken the precaution to paint the roof with a sill:late preparation made by Messrs. Gossage and Sons, Widnes, and to this alone practically the safety of the building was due. Mr. Greenhalgh, business manager of the theatre, stated that the iron curtain was not lowered because of the draught whic,a would be'caused in the flies. "To theatre will not burn," remarked Mr. Kiddie, who con- fid.ntly expects to "stage " the" Derby Winner" on Monday next.

PRESENTATION 10 A RUNCORN SCHOOLMASTER.

At the annual meeting of the Cheshire District Union of the National Union of Teachers, held at Altriocham on Saturday, Mr. J. F. Moulton, head- master of the Runcorn Board Schools, was presented with a beautifully illuminated address by the-member,. of the executive, in recognition of his valuable services to the organisation as member of the Council, president of the Cheshire District Union, and in other capacities. The presentation was made by Dr. Beach, (barrister-tt-law), on behalf of the Council, and he passed a glowing eulop,ium on the meriorious services Mr. Moulton had rendered to the cause. The following is the text of the address : " National Union of Teachers, Cheshire District Union. --T. F. Moulton, E,q.—Your professional brethren of the county rejoice to offer you on the occasion of the expiration of the period of your presidentship an endur- ing expression of their high esteem and profound regard. With conspicuous ability and inflexible impartiality you have directed the consultations of our council, and your extended experience and educational sagacity have largely contributed to the successful results of its de- liberations. In your earnest and constant endeavour to promote general educational advancement you have consistently emphasized the fact that the welfare of the teacher is essential to and inextricably bound up with the well-being of the child. Your successful efforts in the sacred cause of charity will be long remembered, and will be an incentive to us to work on behalf of the sufferers in our midst. Not only your beneficial visits to the Associations of Cheshire demand our recognition, but your able advocacy, at the National Conferences, of principles vital to the moral probity and the industrial i and commercial prosperity of our country merits our grateful appreciation. We trust you will long be spared in health and happiness to your family and profession. Signed: W F Knott, president; Albert J Sidebottom, vice-president; John Wood, treasurer; George Beach, member of executive; Ralph Lees, secretary."

DEATH AND FUNERAL OF MR. THOMAS HUGHES, WIDNES.

The district of Widnes generally, will regret to bear of the death of Mr. Thomas Hughes, shipbEoker of Widnes, which took place at his residence, 19, CRblmondeley-st., on Friday last, after but a comparatively short illness. Mr. Hughes first came to Widnes in the year 1858, as agent for the Holywell Line Company, and Llandilles stone qua.rries, and later commenced business as a shin, broker, in which he met with a considerable measure of success. He was well known amongst the fraternity- and his familiar figure will be sadly missed ; as will also his deeds ot charity and love. Mr. Hughes was a Non conformist and always had large and broad views of Christianity, which be carried out in his daily life. Ihe funeral took place on Tuesday afternoon, and was attended by the relatives and friends of the deceased' who followed the hearse to Farnworth Church. yard in mourning coaches. At the burial ser- vice the Rev. S. Wright Williams (vicar), who officiated referred to the deceased :—He said he should like to add a few words of testimony respect- ing the life of the departed Mr. Thomas Hughes. In doing so he felt that he was but taking part in a duty. His first knowledge of Mr. Hughes commenced when he was pastor of St. Mary's Church, and he could assure them he felt it was an honour to know suchSa good IChristian character as had been borne by the deceased. I Although not of thesame denomination,as far as earthly places of worship went, they were one in their desire reepecting spiritual matters and were both aiming for the same heaven. The more he became acquainted with Mr. Hughes the more he liked him and in following his treatment of the poor and sick of the parish—which was most liberal—he felt they with deepest regret were parted from one who was a pattern, as far as he was permitted of his Master. Mr. Hughes was one of those gentlemen who mane everyone who came in contact with him feel tbe better for his acquaintance. It was but dealing with a small chapter of his life to say he visited the sick ; helped the poor and cared for tho sinner. His love of Christ was preached in his every day life. His was a glorious life. His Christian character was amply displayed by the patient and kindly way he cared for the boy whom he had adopted, and whom he had but a short time Pre- viously buried in that churchyard. It had been a bitter pang to the speaker to hear of Mr. Hughes's death ; and when speaking to him about five weeks ago he little thought he should be so soon called to perform that sad rite. Yet he had a firm conviction that when his own turn came to follow into the great beyond he should not I fail to meet him where the Son of Righteousness I welcomed His redeemed.

The natives of Persia have an odd way of testing a carpet to see if it is a true Persian product. A piece of redhot charcoal is dropped upon it, which leaves a round singed spot. If the carpet is of the first quality the singed wool can be brushed off with the hand, without leaving a trace of the burn discernible. UNDBR THE CLOCK ron 400 D BOOTS. -Aclrt

LANCASHIRE SENIOR COMPETITION.

Warrington v. St. Helens. A Pointless Draw at Wilderspool.

Keen rivals met at W'arrington on Saturday when St. Helens raid a visit to the Wilderspool grounds. Last year the teams met three times (once in a cup tie),War- rington winning and drawing at St. Helens, and losing at home • St.Efelens men Came with the determina- ti3n of repeating last year's feat, and brought about a thousand spectators with them. Warrington boat the toss and Taylor started against the sun, and St Helens getting off-iide they were immediately penned in their own qearters. The Saints now as rted themselves, Traynor carrying play into home. territory where Burgess al most let them in by a wild pass to Isherwood. Law- less and Eden relieved with a splendid dribble the length ofthe fia'd, Warringten having hard luck in not go tting a try, and continued to attack, but the passing was of the most wretched description. The half- backs seemed completely demoralised. Fish ran and kicked across, Traynor stepping into the breach. The Saints combined better behind, and a fine piece of work byLiversaeo saw Warrington driven back, Fish grassing Doherty a yard from the line. They were not allowed to remain here long, for Lawless and Hock- enhall ran well into the other half, the wing man being eventually sent in touch. Play was carried on at a very fast pace, and the teams seemed evenly matched. Warrington were not at their best, and once when Bur- gess broke away beautifully, he was unsupported and fell a victim to Foulkes. Trlynor made,a fine run'in re- turn, Hallam saving luckily right on the line. S. Helens pressed hotly, Pennine°, and Doherte missing easy chances of scoring in less than a couple Of tn;nute3. Albad pass to Traynor sp,iled another beautiful opening, and Warrington had eometbing to be thankful for when they got play into midfield again. Half-time came with the score sheet blank.

Traynor restarted, and early on the Saints got near the home line. Pennington snapped a pass and punting over Hallam's head, there was an exciting MC) for the line, which was won log Isherwood. Warrington cleared by the aid of L twless who secured and ran half the length of the field, Faulkes being called upon in turn. End to end play followed, Mereweither twice tackling Foulkes, after speedy following up Traynor tried a drop at the home goal, but kiek-d wide Burgess and Fish failing at similar efforts. Hockenhall gave Warrington the best chance of the afternoon by running down touch and centring. Isherwood picked up and raced for the line, throwing himself over as Fish stood to take the pass. Unfortunately Isherwood was pulled back, and no try resulted. Alpass to Fish would have made it a cert tinty. St. Helens had to withstand a hot attack, but profited by the temporary absence of Morrison, and cleared their lines. Burgess in tde another great effort, bu,,. tell when near the line, and the match ended pointless, neither side having scored.

ANTI-VACCfNATI(M—Junes Douglas, junk, of Leinster Gardens, applied for a certificate of exemption from vaccina- tion in respect to his child James William Cyril Douglas. The applicant said he '-iad a conscientious objection to vac- cination. After observation, and careful thought he firmly believed vaccina'ion would be prejudicial to the child's heiilth.—Mr Handley said the bench could not go beyond that, hut in these days of the 1138 of antidoxines for the cure of most diseases, it did seem unwise not to accept vaccina- tion. However if the applicant had a conscientious objee- tion, that satisfied the statute and that was all the magis- trates had to do with. The certificate was granted. ASSOCIATION FOOTBALL.

Warrington V. Warrington St. Mary's. The Warrington Association team commenced their season on Saturday at Arpley with a friendly match against the smart local team of St. Mary's. The home team played several new men. The Saints won the toss, and Warrington played with a strong sun in their faces. Riley started, and tha home team imme- diately made tracks for the Saints' goal, but Dil- worth cleared splendidly and a goal from Robinson, close in was ,the outcome of a splendid bit of passing. Eacoura.ed by this early success, the Saints again raided the home territory, but Thompson relieved, and running up on the left, centred Co Murray, who missed a splendid chance to equalise by kicking over the bar. Both ends were now visited in turn, the Saints having the better of the exchanges. Half- time arrived with the Saints leading by one goal to oil. On the resumption of hostiaties Warrington

assumed the aggressive. The Saints tried hard to notch a second point, but faulty play in front of goal accounted fot their failure. The Warringtonians had hard lines with some good shots. The two Manors went up with a rush, and Warrington were sorely pressed. After exchange kicking Hill ran up the centre and passed to Robinson, who had no difficulty in scoring the second point. From the kick off Riley ran away, and Dilworth was forced to kick outside. The Saints temporarily broke away, but the locals returned. George Mather ran up the left, and had hard lines in not scor- ing, he kicking dead. Warrington passed well, but Murray kicked dead. Fleming was applauded for some good play, and Aspinall was twice called upon to save. Riley got going, but experienced hard luck. Stewart had to save, and Warrington kicked dead. Both ends were vigorously assailed, and Richardson had a good shot, which Aspinall just put over the bar. Warrington raided the Saints' citadel, and Stewart saved well. Play continued on the half-way line. Riley was conspisuous with a clever run, whilst Thompson experienced hard luck with a good shot. Final t—St• Mary's two goals; Warrington nil.

NEWTON V. OSWESTRY COMBINATION. —o3Wee.try United played their second game 10 the Combination tournament on Saturday, when they had Newton-le- Willows as opponents. The start was a late one, but of a rather sensational descriptioz.. No sooner had the ball been put in motion than Gladstone Davies got possession and, racing along the right win, worked his way well into the Newton quarters; when close in be centred to Mark Watkins, and the latter player, getting his head under the ball, beat Horrocks, and scored the first goal of the match. This success, coming in the first minute of the game, pleased the large crowd immensely, and also acted as a stimulus to the team, with the result that ere the finish they had increased their total to four. The winners, all round, played a plucky and fast game. F Benton, the captain, took his place at centre half, and, as usual, fed his forwards with judg- ment, at the same time keeping them well in Land. Benbow at back was in his best humour, some of his clearances being very smart. The whole team deserve a word of praise, as from beginning to end touts were of very rare occurrence. Newton, who on the previous Saturday accounted for Tranmere Rovers, are a level, well-balanced lot, and their forwards more than once made Tracey's positioa a very uncomfortable one. The right wing, Kenny and Butier, are a bard-working pair Morrissey, in the centre, sent in two or three hard shots which the home custodian was lucky in clearing, while the left wing pair. Horton and Lee, rendered a good account of themselves.

LANCASHIRE LEAGUE.

Earlestown Coming to the Front.

Fresh from their victory over Darwen. the Earles- town team visited Chorley, where they accomplished a very meritorious performance in beating the Dole- laners, who had beenj playing:exceedingly well up to then. The Charley team included two new men in Nunwick, centre half, who hails from Burnley, and Chorlton, a forward recruit from Bolton Wanderers. The weather was mact e..mgenial and 3,000 spectators lined the enclosure. Both teams appeared to be very evenly matehel for the first half-hour, but at length the home lot had a free kick for a foul, and Green, placing this well, Huxley secured in the goal-mouth and beat Chew. Towards half-time Earlestown retaliated, and the ball was punted in the goal-mouth. Bennett missing it in rushing out, and though Oatick tipped away, it was returned, Joe Green then hitting it with his fist. The resultant penalty kick was productive of an equalising goal, scored by Settle. The resumption after the interval saw matters keenly exciting although the play was a trifle rough, players on both sides being to blame. Within five minutes Banks gave Chorley the lead from a scrimmage, but in the end Earlestown retired the winners by three goals to two. The game was of a scrappy rushing character, and, taking the play altogether, there was little to choose between the two elevens. Tee Chorley men certainly showed a great falling away from recent form, their half back line being decidedly weak, and this seemAl to have a deteriorating, effect on the backs, who at various points were shaky and lacked confidence. In the for- ward line James Green, Huxley. and Banks combined very satisfactorily. Chew, for Earlestown effected some remarkable saves, and the backs played with much determination. A most effective wing were Settle and Leach, while the half-backs—Pennington. Green, and Whitehouse--gave admirable support to their forward line.

BOWLING.

Closing Main at The —Conservative Club.

On Saturday, September Bth, the members of the Conservative Bowling Club held their closing main, and on Saturday last the consolation game,for the beautiful prizes which had been outscribecl tor by the members of the club,was played. The main resulted in Mr James Holland winning the first prize, Mr J Millington the secand. Mr R Anderton the third, Mr W Kerfoot the fourth and Mr T Cooper the fifth. Mr W Davies sec- needed in winning the premier prize in the consolation match, Mr W Hill the second. Mr W Strettle the third, Mr S Caldwell the fourth,Mr Jonn Harrison the fifth,rdr G Watkins the sixth, Mr P 13radburne the seventh, and Mr T Sutton the eighth. The prizes were distributed on Saturday evening by Councillor John Roberts, the chair- man of the Bowling Green Committee. Votes of thanks were accorded the chairman, the subscribers, B3wling Green Committee, the secretary and the manager.

Should Warrington have Municipal

Bowling Greens?

The Council and The General Election.

There was a good attendance at the monthly meeting of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council which-- was held at the Waggon and Horses Inn, Buttermarket street, on Tuesiay evening. Mr. A C Bmks presided and the attendance included the secretary (Mr. William Bullock) and the treasurer (Mr. J Battershy).

THE HOUSING OF THE WORKING CLASSES AND CHEAP

TRAINS FOR WORKMEN.

A communication wAs received trorn. ttie jaint secre- taries of the Conference recently held at Huddersfield with reference to the housing ot the working classes and cheap trains for workingnaan, enclosing copies of resolu- tions passed at the Coaterencs,

The resolutions, which were read to the Council by the secretary were to the effect that the conference strongly urged the organised workmen in every locality to form themselves into focal committees for the purpose of forcing tneir local administrative bodies to make as much use as possible of the powers contained In the Housing of the Working Classes lets, 1890 to 1900, and particularly where there was a scarcity of house accommodation, to buy land and build coev and good houses for working people ; such local committee's where people have to riue to and from their work, to make representations to the Board of Trade with the object of obtaining improved travelling facilities and cheaper fares under toe provisions of the Trams Act. 1883. The conference pledged itself to a united work men's movement to secure the ,desired reforms, and trusted that in. the coming General Election every working class elector would vote only for those candidates who would definitely promise to vote and work strongly for the reforms outlined in the two Bills.

The Council passed a rrellutiou expressing their entire sywpathy with the resolution, and decided to put questions with regard to the matters Co the local candi- dates in the General Election.

A NEW MEETING Room FOR THE COUNCIL. The SECRETARY reported that at the last meeting the matter of the proposed new meeting room was discussed. He wrote to the Town Clerk with regard Co a room in the Town Hall, and in reply received a letter stating that the ;Mayor would be pleased ,to meet a deputation on Wednesday evening, the 12th inst. The deputation- waited upon his Worship, and he (the secretary) would ask the Chairman to state what tock place. The CHAIRMAN informed the meeting that the deputa- tion was very courteously received by the Mayor. They explained the nature ot their business, and the Mayor whilst regretting that the Council were not in a position to allow them the use of a room at present said that their petition would be filed and probably at some future period when the municipal buildings were more convenient, ths matter would be again considered. One objection they had to allowing the Council the use ef the Town Hall was that the building was fully occupied by the officials at present and they had hardly sufficient room for themselves. Another objection was that if they gave the Council permission to meet at the Town Hall they would be creating a precedent whereby other public bodies would acquire the,same right and privilege to use the room for their meetings. Mr Stott instanced a number of places where Trades Councils met in munici- pal buildings and also gave the charge for the use of the rooms. The authorities, however, seemed to have made a determined stand that they could not allow the Council to use the Town Hall.

Mr. Berrxesev said tint the Mayor referred to the fact that the Infirmary governors held meetings at the Town Hall and the deputation said they would be very sorry if any action on their part,prevented them having the use of the Hall. Personally he felt indebted to the Mayor for his kind treatmene of the deputation. Mr. PLINSTON : Can you tell us how it is that the Mayor received the deputation and the committee which was approached did not P—The Mayor by virture of his office only is a member of the committee. The CHAIRMAN : In regard to that we have no in— formation. We have had no communication whatever from the Town Hall, Park and Baths Committee. Mr. BATTERSBY ; I think the Mayor expressed their opinion.

-Mr. STOTT : I think before the Mayor' arranzed.to meet the deputationshe had been instructed. Continui.ng he said he felt fully satisfied with the explanation which the Mayor gave them.

Mr. EDWARDSON thought that good came from the matter and perhaps the Council would be considered on some future occasion. On the motion of Mr. Plinston seconded by Mr. Rath' bone the secretary was instructed to write to the Mayor and,Town Clerk thanking them for the courteous manner in which they received the deputation. The CHAIRMAN said that after seeing the Mayor tbe•,, deputation took the opportunity of visiting the Druiu Hall and found that the large room was capable of being divided intoo two sections, one of which would com• fortably accommodate all the members and also ViSitOre• The price asked for was very low. Several other members of the Council endorsed et:: Chairman's remarks and it was decided that the r°° - be engaged for future meetings. PROPOSED MUNICIPAL BOWLING GREENS. The CHAIRMAN stated that the deputatioll also . referred to the matter of the .municipal bowling gree.,t being established in Warrington. The Mayor ‘" gave :1 suggestion his entire sympathy and advised the Council to petition the Town Council and also to get particutars. as to the charges for and management of ,:municipal bowling greens in other towns. He (the chairman) agreed with what to Mayor said with regard to the petition and the obtaining of information. It would not only be profitable to the Borough to have bowling greens but it would also encourage sobriety. It WAS decided that the secretary should write ccl obtain the required information. THE COUNCIL AND THE GENERAL ELECTI9N. Mr. PLINSToN said it was very probable that a. political contest would take place before the nest monthly meeting of the Council. Ele asked if it would not be wise in that event that the Council should be called together for the purpose of dealing with the questions to be submitted to the candidates who might " put up" for the position of Parliamentary Member for the Borough.

Mr. BArr- ERSBY asked what mAtters the questionS would relate to?

Mr. PLINSTON : To trade union matters. Mr. SToTT : The resolution we have passed tollight pledges every member to support the candidate wt" will promise to support those measures. The CuaissfAN said it would be for the secretary and himself to call the meeting. Mr. Wit.i.cociti : I understood the Council had nothing whatever to do with politics. The CAAIRmAN : We are not dealing with politics 14 all. Mr. PLINSTON : It is not a matter of Tory and Radical. It is a matter of vital:importance. Mr. Wit.r.cocils : Why should we bring it up now that the election is on ?

Mr. RATHBORNE : BentlSO we wish to know %dile' candidates will support our clauses. Mr. WILLCOCKS . It must be politics then. The CHAIRMAN : It is economics not politics. It was decided that a sp(cial meeting should be called if necessary.

THE EXAMINER, FRIDAY, SEPTEMBER 21, 19OG MR. A. H. CROSFIELD IN

===== 0002601_19000921_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
AMERICA. A Yankee's Description of him at Golf,

Mr. A. H Crosfield is gaining fresh laurels as a golf player in America. In the second day's play at Newport on August 29th, he scored a big success in beating R U Watson, jun.' and in describing the match the "Providence Journal" says :—" The second day's play, the first of match play, at the INewport Golf Club's annual invitation 'tournament, brought its surprise, and a big one. This was the defeat in yesterday afternoon's round of R C IWatson, jun., picked by all the knowing ones as the man who should carry away the President's cup, by a rank outsider, an Englishman, who has never before played in a tour- nament in this country, but who will henceforth be looked on with great respect by followers of golf. All Crosfield is his name. He is a member of the Hoylake Club, near Liverpool, England, and he has been spending Ithis summer touring this country. Earlier in the season he spent a fortnight at Newport and played overate° Newport Club's coure Then he journeyed to the west, where he played at the Onwentsia Club, but not in any tour- naments. He returned to Newport on Tuesday in time to get into the qualifying round of the tourna- ment, his card placing him sixth of the 16 who qualified, Watson being first with a score of 155 against Crosfield's 167. Tee morning round lessened the competitors for the President's cup to eight, and everybody around the grounds was ready to pick a winner. The general opinion was that it lay between Watson, Hitchcock, and Byers, with the chances about two to one in favour of Watson carrying the cup home with him. Consequently, when he started out, the first of the players for the President's cup to the up in the afternoon, a good sized gallery was on hand to follow the match. There was a general feeling that he had an easy thing with his English opponent, of whom, it appeared, nobody had ever heard before. Mr. Crosfield presented to the critical American eye, a peculiar appearance. His hands were encased in white kid gloves, new and snowy; his coat was off, exposing his "gallusses " which supported his high water white flannel trousers,and his English accent was a thing to turn the Anglo-maniac, if any there were among his followers, green with envy. His caddy built his tees for him, evidently in order that the white kids should not suffer. Crosfield took the honour, and drove a low ball I well toward the green, while Watson begin the ' match by topping, his ball landing in the cleft of a bunker, from which he recovered himself well, Crosfield standing away after Watson's second stroke. The first mishap lost the ball for Watson. however, Crosfield taking it in 4 to his 5* On the second Watson pulled his second stroke and the ball struck a rock, sending him considerably out of direction,his opponent playing straight for the hole which he took in a 5. The third went to Watson, following a pretty iron drive with a running approach with his putter, a favourite play of his and holing out on his third. Crosfield gave a fore- taste of the admirable approach work he is capable of, but missed an easy putt. Crosfield's approaches were of the orthodox type, made with a mashie or other lofting iron, always excellent in direction and dropping almost dead on the green. After referring to various other movements in the game, the writer continues "The fact that the Englishman was four up at the end of the first nine holes was considerable of a surprise to the gallery but there was still hope that Watson was only lay- ing back and would now show what he was capa- ble of doing. At this point Crosfield took it into his head to show what he could do. The hole is a short one of 181 yards, and his drive took him, to the green, about 25 feet from the hole From there he ran his ball down as easily as anything was ever done, making the hole in 2, a feat which was generously applauded. by the spectators. the Englishman politely taking off his cap in acknowledgment. On the next hole, Watson took a brace, and made it in bogey figure, while Crosfield sliced his third stroke and fell down on a long putt. The 12th should also have been Watson's but he used three strokes on the green, and the hole was halved. Crosfield gave him another chance on the 13th by duplicating his opponent's performance on the preceding green, but Watson's drive sent him into a sand pit, the result being the hole was halved in 4. Two good drives on the 14th were followed on Watson's part by a beautiful long brassie, and a long putt by Crosfield was the only thing which prevented Watson from taking the hole.

Crosfield was four up and Watson needed all the rest to tie the match and give himself a chance to win on an extra. He started the 15th by slicing a long drive into ,high grass and weeds, one of the worst places beside the course. Crosfield made a beautiful drive getting in admirable position for a brassie approach, but his brassie was pulled into as bad a place as that in which Watson found himself. Watson's second stroke went into a ditch, finding lodgment on the edge from which he played out with a wooden putter. The combination of troubles proved too much for him, aud CrosfUd took the hole in 6, and with it the match. Their cards. Crosfield Out In Watson, Out In 4, 5,5, 4, 5,6, x, x. x,

===== 0002601_19000921_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
Warrinatan Electricity Scheme.

Progress of the Works.

Having endu—red for many months the displea- sures and inconveniences of having our footpaths and streets torn up in order to allow mains to be laid for the electric supply, it is somewhat of a relief to know that at last the work is practically completed. The question naturally arises, when is , the electric light going to be turned on? This query cannot be answere6 with any definiteness at the present time inasmuch as the electricity works are still far from being in a complete state. With a view to obtaining further informa- tion on the matter, a reporter of the "Examiner," visited the works yesterd‘y. He there found about ISO man busily engaged in various operations. Rapid progress is being made with the work, and so far as Mr C Vir Davenport's portion is concerned, it may be stated that be has now nearly,completed it. All the main erections are finished as well as the tall chimney which has already become a local land mark. 44*

The chimney is 100 feet in height and has a width of ten feet at the base and nine feet at the top. It was with some trepidation that our repre- sentative accepted an invitation to ascend this huge pile of masonry. The means for doing so are not of the most comfortable or reassuring kind to the mere outsider. Having taken a seat in a wooden trough you are given strict instructions to hold fast to the leading rope with the left hand and guide yourself by means of a rope with the right. The signal is then given and the ascent corn- menm. To find oneself gradually rising from the ground with only a rope to depend upon is eaten- liited to increase one's love tor Mother Earth. Slowly but surely the ascent continues until at last a height of ILO feet is reached. Oa arriving at the top of this column of brickwork,our representative found that the last coping stone had been laid, and when the lightning conductor has been attached, the chimney will be complete. From the top a fine bird's eye view of the town and district was obtain- able, but it was with some relief that the descent was safely accomplished and terra firma once more reached.

Considerable plant has been laid down in some of the buildings, In the boiler house, Messrs Bab- cock and Wilcox have erected boilers and other plant, and this section is nearing completion. In another portion of the works, refuse destructors are in course of erection, and a paved roadway is being constructed leading up to them. The engine house is in the most backward state. The structure is completed but no plant has yet been put down. This will take some considerable time and it is therefore prema- ture to fix any date for the formal opening of the works. From what can already be seen there are good grouuds for believing that when finished the Corporation will be in possession of the finest electric lighting station in the country. Every modern convenience, adapted from the principal stations in existence, has been introduced.

The Syllabus for next Session.

The syllabus for the Session 1900-1931 has just been issued on behalf of the Museum and 'lectinical I ustruc- tion Committee by Mr. Charles Madeley, the secretary. As will be seen trora the advertisement in another column the classes will re-apen next Monday evening, and the syllabus gives complete information as to the subjects taught, amount of fees, prizes, &3. It should certainly be consulted by all intending students and can be obtained fram the Museum at be price of one penny (by post 2,1)• TEE SCHOOL OF ART.

The objects of this school are to promote the growth of industrial art, to make dra a log a part of general education, and to train thoss who desire to follow art as a profession. The session commences on Monday and will close on July 13th. The evening classes have been arranged to meet the new regulations in regard to the elementary teachers' certificate. There will be the usual elementary and advanced classes in art, including applied art. There will also be special classes in wood- carving. The head master (Mr. C. B. Aylward) will be pleased to advise students in the selection of classes suited to their individual requirements. The Museum and Technical Instruction Committee offers to evening students five scholarships on the result of the examina• tion work done in the School of Art durisg the session. The value of the scholarships will be 103 (elementary) and 15s (advanced) and will be tenable at the school next session. Two scholarships, value £3 3s each, are offered to day students on similar terms. A , special examination in drawing is held at the elemens tary schools in Warrington in September, and free ad- mission to the School of Art for one year is granted to all who pass. In addition there are the Charles Middle- , ton scholarships open to boys from elementary schools in Warrington. Senior Scholarships, of the value of £9 first year, £l2 second year, £l5 third year, together with day fees at the School of Act, are awarded on the result of the examinations of the science and Art De- partment. Candidates must be under 17, are required to attend the School three days weekly, and to attend classes in History, Literature, and Languages. Junior Scholarships, value £4 first year, £5 second year, £6 third year, together with the School or Art fees, are awarded by competition at the special examination in September, open to all boys exempt from the obligation tolattend school' Scholars are required to attend the School of Art three evenings weekly, and also an ap- proved day school. Mr F W Monks offers a Scholarship value £5, to girls, under similar conditions to thcse of the Charles Middleton Junior Scholarships. The Lan- cashire County Council offers annually 25 exhibitions and four scholarships in art, to residents in Lancashire outside the Borough of Warrington. Prizes are also offered by the Science and Art Department, the Union of Lancashire and Cheshire Institutes, the British Institution, the Gilchrist Educational Trust, and the Society for the encouragement of arts,manufactures and commerce.
