SCHOOL OF SCIENCE.

The school of science, for which a new entrance is provided in Museum-street, contains a chemical labora- tory well supplied with appliances for work in the ad- vanced and honours stages, metallurgical laboratory, physical laboratory, wood working shop and cookery room. The following new subjects have been added ; practical mathematics, botany, and nologs'. Hygiene re-appears in place of the advanced stage of physiology. The new classes which were opened last session are con- tinued, and arrangements have been made ta re-open toe class in tanning. These additions have involved some ehanges in the time table. Class prizes wi I be awarded by the teachers on the general home work and trial examinations in the follow ing subjects. Two prizes value 7s 63 and 5s will be given in each subject or group, the committee reserving power to withhold one or both in case of insufficient competition :—pure mathematics, practical mathematics, theoretical me- chanics, geometry, building construction, machine drawing, theoretical chemistry, practical chemistry, electricity, iron and steel, tanning, applied me- chanics and steam, physiology, ziology, and botany. The Museum and Technical Instruction Committee offer a scholarship (tenable at the institute next session) to each student who complies with the rules of the school, and takes a first class in the spring examinations. The value of a scholarship is 5s for the elemeatary stage,and 103 for the advanced stage. The holder of an elemen- tary scholarship will be required to take the advanced stage (if a class in provided) of the subject in which the scholarship was gained, or a closely allied subject, asd the holder of an advanced scholarship to take a group of allied subjects approved by the committee. Scholars will pay the class fees as usual, and the amount of the scholarships will be paid on their attend- ing two-thirds of the class meetings and sitting (if re- quired) at the examination. The County Council of Lancashire offer annually scholarships (value £6O yearly for three years), and exhibitions (value £10) for profi- ciency in science or commercial subjects.Tne next exam- inations will be held probably in April,l9ol and are open to residents in Lancashire outside the borough-The Union of Lancashire and Cheshire Institutes awards pr.zes (1) to successful candidates in subjects in which examina- tions are held by the union, (2) on the result of certain of the science examinations, (3) for sets of mechanical and architectural drawinss. The union also has the disposal of valuable scholarships to successful science students. The Board of Education also offer Queen's prizes of books, Ste. to a small number of those who, having previously passed in the elementary Stage, do best in the advanced stage of each subject. Prizes are also given for sets of inked-in draw- ings sent up by students in machine or building construction. Royal exhibitions of £5O a year, national scholarships value 30s a week, free studentships at tbe Royal Collage of Science, and Whitworth scholarships ' are awarded on the result of the May examinations. For conditions, see the directory of the Department, at the Museum.

HARVEST FESTIVALS.

[Continued from page 3.1 St. Paul's, Runcorn.

The harvest festival at St. Paul's Church, Runcorn, was again this year remarkable for its heartiness and simplicity. The service commenced on Sunday, the preacher being, in the morning the Rev. H. Morrison, and in the evening the Rev. Alfred Wells (superinten- dent). With their usual thoroughness, the capable choir had given special attention to the musical portion of the service, and the singing throughout was of a high order. The decorations of the sacred edifice were of tne same standard as has characterised previous years, and were carriel out by a large number of ladies and gentlemen. members of the congregation under the superintendence of :—Mrs Robinson, the pulpit; Miss Norman and Miss Lightfoot, the Communion rail ; Mrs Marshall, baskets; Mrs Ellison, pillars ; Mrs Simkin, Miss Povah, and Mrs Bisbrown, windows ; Mrs Morris, choir stalls, etc.—The es'..itial was conciauld on I.)nd,a y evening, the pupit ein4 occupied by th Rev B arnott Foster. rhe festival hrltighout was well attended and the collectioas realised £l7 5s 9d.

===== 0002601_19000921_art0145.txt | included=true | included =====
Legh-street Primitive Methodist Chapel Warrington.

The Harvest Thanksgiving SerVices Were held on Sun- dae last at Legh-street Primitive Methodist Chapel, and were throughout a distinct success. Tne chapel had been tastefully de3orated, largely by the young people, and the services were well attended. At the afternoon and evening services it was difficult to find accomoda- tion for all corners. The morning and evening serviees were conducted by the Rev. 14' Jeffs, of Preston, whose sermons were filled with thoughts which naturally arise in the mind at the season of harvest. He showed with great clearness how the operations of the agriculturist are symbolic of proc,sses in the religious life ; and although man may forsn theories on the conditions of growth and development ot plant,fl )wer,and fruit,he cannot get away from the conception of an all-directing God,whosedefinite and unchanging plans man calls laws. We arguments were irresistible,and his illustrations apt ; and through- out he carried the congregation with him. During the evening service the choir sang the anthem" Thou visiter the earth" (Calcott) and Beethoven'a chorus "Hallelujah," from the " Mount of Olives." Much credit is due to the choir for their fine renderings,more especi- ally of Beethoven's "Hallelujah." The chorus is of considerable difficulty, but the choir performed it quite correctly and with jest expression. The parts were taken up with precision, and the blending of the voices was paricularly to be remarked. The service in the afternoon, presided over by Councillor W F Forshaw, was entirely of a musical character. The soloists were Madame F. Christian (soprano), who sang " The Promise of Life 'and " Two Cities." Mr. Levi Richardson (tennr),voho gave “Suffer the Children" and" Come unte Me," and Mr. E. H. Pearce (baritone). who sang "The City of the King "and "Po Peccatia." Miss Bridge took the solo in the anthem " Rock of Ages" (Buck). We cannot speak too highly of the soloists, for each was in fine voice, and gave great pleasure to their hearers. The thanks of the officials of the chapel were heartily given to each for their valuable services. The success of the service, both musically and financially, is without doubt very largely owing to their help. In additioa to the anthem already named, the choir sang Batson's " They that sow in tears," and Haydn's "Heavens are telling." All the anthems were we! rendered ;. and when this remark is applied to Haydns difficult chorus, the high state of proficiency of the choir can be estimated. The service was entirely arranged by Mr. A Spruce, the leader of the choir, who has reason to be gratified with its success. Mr. F Podmore very ably performed the arduous duties of organist at all tne services. It is not yet known what the nett proceedings will be, but it is certain that the amount realised will be much in advance of former years.

JUBILEE OF BOLD-STREET WESLEYAN CHAPEL.

The Mayoral Visit To-Morrow.

The celebration of the jubilee of Bald street Wesleyan Chapel will take place toinsorrow (Sunday), when the occasion will be fittingly recognised. The Rev. F. W. Macdonald, the ex-president of the Wesleyan Conference will preach morning and evening. He has long been recognised as one of the most distinguished ministers of the Wesleyan Church. His gifts are so unique and brilliant that there is no need to emphasise them. He is a master ofilanguage and is recognised as one of the most effective speakers in this country. He belongs to a most distinguished family. His father was the late Rev. G. B. Macdonald, one of the leading Wes- leyan Methodists of his day, who preached at the opening of Bold-street Church in 1850. He is also the uncle of Rudyard Kipling, who is the grandson of a Wesleyan minister, both on the paternal and the maternal side. His two sisters married such distinguished Royal Academicians as Sir Edward Burne Jones and Sir Edward John Poynter. Opportunities of hearing him preach in this district are so rare that many will no doutst avail themselves of the opportunity which is now presented. The Mayor has issued the following invitations to the memb3rs of the Council :

"Town Hall, Warrington, 18th September, 1900. Dear Sir, I am informed that on Sunday September 23rd, the congregation worshipping at Bold Street Chapel will celebrate the Jubilee of that place of Worship. The es- President of the Wesleyan Conference the Rev. F W Macdonald will be the preacher on the occasion and I have been requested by the Superintendent Minister; the Rev Theodore Bishop to invite the members of the Council to accompany me on Sunday morning to Divine °iship. As the occasion is a very important one and is in c3nnection with a denomination many of the members of which have at various times taken a large and important part in the affairs of our town and the surrounding district, and as the denomination is one itself which has rendered great service to the cause of religion in our country I think the occasiou deserves official recognition in the manner indicated. I shall therefore be glad if you can make it con- venient to attend Divine Service with me on the day in question leaving the Town Hall at 10 a.m.—Yours faithfully. H. ROBERTS, Mayor. . . _ _ _

In the afternoon there will bs a great gathering of young peopin, when an addruss will he delivered by the Rev. H. G. Roberts, M.ti. Mr. R. Garnett, (Tx., will preside.

ketttrs to tbt Obitor.

Some Anomalies of National Religion. Sir,—l thank you for publishing my previous letter in "The Examiner "of the 25th of August, and now res- pectfully submit some further rem irks on the subject which, if approved, I trust may find a place in your journal. Til2 world has never been without religion. From the days of Adam, in all ages, and in all countries the minds of men have been more or less occupied in endeavours to find out God. The grandeur and beauty of the world in which they lived, and the glories of the %tarty heavens above them naturally created a &sirs to know something of the almighty and invisible Being, who had designed and formed all these things. But an impenetrable cloud of montal darkness obscured their vision and depraved thAr intellect. And having no standard but their own hearts to guide their specula- tions they concluded that, however high and mighty God might be, he must have parts and passions similar to their own. And hence it was that they associated him with their battleq,and even went so far as to believe that the slaughter of their fellow creatures was pleasing in His sight. This diabolical creed has lived through all the ages, and, notwithstanding the advances of civiliza. tion, it still finds a place among all the nations of the earth.

But a new era dawned upon the worhi when Jesus of Nazareth contlimed the dream of the Hebrew prophets and announced the glorious doctrine that the maker and tbe ruler of the universe was a God of love. And in his own person Jesus himself was an embodi- ment of that love ; the sole aim and purpose of his life and death being to glorify God, and promote peace and good will among men. No word ever fell from His sacred lips to countenance animosity, and the shedding of human blood; but both by precept and personal example be taught his lowly followers to cherish brotherly love amongst themselves, and to extend tb-at love even to their enemies. That they loyally and lovingly followed in tbe footsteps of their beloved Master must be obvious to every reader of the Sew Testa- ment. John, the beloved disciple, was the only one of the Apostles who did not seal his mission with his blood; and Jerome tells us that his usual salutation to the Church at Ephesus was, "Little children love one another." The same gentle and beautiful spirit sems to have inspired his disciple Ignatius. For in his epistle to Polycarp we find such counsels as these "Be studious of unity, than which nothing is more precious. Bear with all men, even as our Lord beareth with thee. Draw out thy spirit to all men in love, as indeed thou doest. If thou Invest the good disciple only, thou bast no grace: seek to overcome those that are evil by gentleness." Such were the views of the early followers of the Lord regarding the duty of Christians to one another and to the world areund them. Selfish and degenerate as the Christian nations are as a whole, at present the blessed doctrine of love to God and man has not yet been wholly suppressel. Multitudes there are, no doubt, in all the countries which profess the faith of Jesus, who in a quiet way endeavour " to do good to all men, hut especially to them that are of the household of faith."

But when we consider the nominally Christian nations as such in relation to the cardinal doctrine of the Gespel we are brought face to face with a state of things that looks very like a practical denial of the doctrine alto• gether. It is true that all these nations make a loud profession of discipleship and employ multitudes of priests and pastors to preach the gospel of the Prince of Peace in cathedrals and ether fine buildings erected for the purpose. But alas, in all the great centres of popu- lation,and almost side by side with the ecclesiastica structures rise huge barracks where men drawn from the peaceful avocations of life by conecription and otherwise are trained and armed for the avowed purpose , of killing their fellow creatures and looting their pro- perty at :be instigation of Christian rulers and states- men. To every unprejudiced mind this militarism must appear its a horrible outrage upon the gospel, if not indeed an:open and undisguised repudiation of its teach- ing. But militarism is more than this. The upkeep of fleets and armies involves an incalculable amount of oppression upon the working claseee. For let no one ' suppose for a moment that it is the wealthy people who And the money for the fighting departments of the Chris- tian states.ll.o men are only monopolists of the earnings of their poorer brethren. It is out of the wages of field labourers, miners, artizens, factory workers, and other toilers in the humble ranks of life that the war funds are drawn. And year by year this unjust and horrible tax upon industry is growing larger and larger. The Christian rulers and statesmen are so suspicious of one another's motives that they are continually adding to their armaments, and thus heaping more burdens upon the well nigh exhausted toilers. And another cruel and unrighteous feature of war expenditure is that large por- tions of it ere thrown forward to burden other genera- tions who have no voice in the matter.and to whom the legacy must aver appear as one to be ashamed of. The financiers for the sword and their royal masters thus openly flout the sound economic principle of the Apostle to "owe no man anything, but to love one another." When the Christian nations are not fighting amongst , themselves they are sure te find employment for their armies elsewhere. A short time ago the inhabitants of Kumasi, the capital of Ashanti, thinking themselves agerieved and wronged by some acts of tlaeir alien governor made an attempt to shake off the foreign yoke. But of course they failed to accomplish their purpose for what savage power could stand against the well disciplined troops of a great Christian empire ! And now their audacity in daring to be rebels in their own country is being energetically chastised by the Christian soldiers who are scouring up aad down their territories, shooting men and women, and burning their 1 villages I Then look for a moment at the present military muddle in China. The people of that vast and uniqueempire have always shown a strong aversion to foreigners. Their own fields and rivers, and th,nr local trading yield them an abundant supple-of the noces- eerie; and luxuries of life, and they preter to live in their own way and to worship their own gods. All this seems fair and patriotic eneueli but unfortunately for the Chinese, it is °pees ,d to the rapacity and ambition of their Christian nei4hirrur3 in the west, and for this reason they have not been able to maintain their isolation. Two examples will suffice to show the character of the aggressions that have, from time to, time been made upon them. Prior to 1840 the abominal opium traffic was strongly condemned by the Chinese authorieies on the ground that it was ruining many of their people. And their opposition to it culminated in the seizure and destruction of several cargoes of the drug in that year. This was an insult which our Christian government could not endure. The traffic in opium was very profitab'e to British traders, however disastrous it might be to the people of China,and so British bayonets forced their way up to Canton, and there settled the dispute by compelling the Chinese to admit the obnoxi- ous drug. The traffic still continues, and the Christian government of India stands in the disgraceful position of adding nine or ten millions a year to its revenue by this method of debauching the heathen. The other Chinese grievance to which I will refer is this. During the past few years there has been a constant and malignant rivalry carried on by the Christian powers of Russia, England, Ger- many, and France for slices of Chinese territory, and large slices have been appropriated in the most shameful manner. New the Chinese, like the Boers, strongly object to the filching of their property by strangers and they are equally averse to having their spiritual con- cerns looked after by missionaries sent to them by the people who pollute both their minds and bodies with opiumMilence their desire to drive the foreigners and their missionaries out of their country altogether. The Christian powers having, by their unchristian acts, sown the wind in China are likely to reap the whirlwind there with a vengeance. On the whole, then, does it not seem that the time has come when the so-called Christian counties should delete the adjective from their names. For surely it is neither more nor less than a stupendone mockery to apply that blessed word to people who are constantly mixed up in the iniquities and horrors of war—an institution aa much opposed to the gospel of Christ as hell is to heaven There it; one more branch of my subject upon which I should like to make BOMA remarks. but I must leave tbem over to a future occasion.—Yours truly, lONA.

September Bth, 1900. PAIMATE.

• INDUCTION OF' THE NEW VICAR ,—On Tuesday evening the Rev. W. Pollock-Hill was induoted to the living of Christ Church, Padgate, by the Ven• Arch- deacon Madden (Archdeacon of Warrington). The service was well attended. The usual form in an induction service was gene through, the Psalms Fun, being the 15th and the 84th, and the lessons Ez,kiel xxxiii. 1-10 and Acts xx. 17-36, which were read respectively by the Rev. Canon 'Willis and the Rev. Canon Penrhyn. The choir rendered the anthem, "They shall mount upon wings as eagles." Preaching from the words " He that holdeth the seven stars in his right hand, who walketh in the mid3t of the seven golden candlesticks," Rev ii. 1, the Archdeocon at the close of his' discourse, reminded his hearers that the church was planted in that place to give forth the light of th, gospel and the glory of Jesus Christ. On the Eddystone Light- house, that great structure which had stood the storms and tempests of many years, were inscribed the words "To Rive light and to save life." So that 'church, that sanctuary of God, should be the true light amidst the storms and tempests of men's Jives. He believed that what the church wanted that day more than anything else was her realization of the actual preserce of the living Christ in her midst. He commended the new vicar of the parish to their prayers and sympathy. Like priest, like people; like people, like nriest. If they were what they ought to be there would be a mutual blessing, for he, bleosing them in his ministry of the Gospel, would receive at their hands blessings through the prayers of the people. And so God would establish His work in the parish and in that Waco, ard in the days to come the bride should be broupht home in triumph to her Lord, and they and the Vicar should be remembered with God's saints in , the glory of the everlasting life.

I been fighting against us with his father, aud ; had been ill ever since. Next we marcned to Reitfontein, where I had been formerly for some time with ten oqier men, Sunday morning we left at 8.30 and marched in here, having done about 110 miles altogether. It WAS a successful patrol, as we captured the chief rebel we were sent after. Captain Daniel thought we might have come in for some fight- ing, but there was no one to b 3 seen near Kheis. Two of the natives brought in our captured sheep. Tney always caught us up each night After we had halted. I enjoyed it very much, mid the native ponies (we all ride them) stood it well. I was very glad of the meat lozenges when we marched from 6.30 to 11.30 without food. We havo had no bad news Vx :apt that De Wet has got away again. The weather ie warmer and very nice now. I have just got a wonderful pair of corduroy trousers. I boughs the stuff at the store and had them made at a cottafve in about two hours. A few of us had a dinner at the store last night, the first meal at a table since leaving aCapotown. Menu :—Olives, soup, fish salad, chickens, roast mutton, apricot pudding, cheese, dessert, coffee, sherry and bitters. white Rhine wine, port, whiskey. It was very well snved up by the people at the store, which is a sort of hotel. Two of the men from the store dined with M. We finished up with songs, and got an extension of leave of absence from camp."

The Burial of Colonel McCarthy-Oleary,

Who was killed whilst gallantly leading -his Regiment

at Pieter' s Hill, On the 27th February, 1900.

Ring out will bells and tell the world Of the vicr.ory we have had. That victory it was dearly bought, And a thousand hearts are sad. We cannot mingle with the crowd, And give a British cheer. One has gone from us for ever, That one we held so dear. No °alien coffin covers him, No Union Jack wrapped round him. No loving wife to lay him out, We buried him as we found him. And a thousand warriors sod around, Each with uncovered head, And not a dry eye was there, Our gallant leader was dead. With wonder in our eyes we stood, As around him we avembled, Is that the man who the day before Made the Boers:tremble ? IV. Yeq, that still form lying there, Going to rest eternal, And beloved by all his Regiment, He wasuour gallant Colonel.

Wlaid him gently in hii e, And our hearts were filled with pain, We knew that our Brave Hero ! Will never lead us again. So farewell you gallant soldier, You warrior brave and true, And a sad farewell from a thousand hearts, For we all adored you. Written and Composed hv No. 2637, Lance-Corporal J. CARTER, Ist South Lancashire Regiment, Natal Field Force.

, MILITARY NOTES.

Second Lieutenants Gaskell, Vince, Paine, and Lardner, of the Liverpool Regiment, have been pro- moted to the rank of Lieutenant in the regiment in which they have served only a few months, for they were all appointed to their first commissions in the corps only last spring. Lieutenant Robinson, of the 3rd Battalion Cheshire Regiment, has been given a commission as Second-Lieutenant in the Army Service Corps. He was only appointed to the old Ist Cheshire Militia last March.

Captain Hopkins is about to give up tile adjut- ancy of the 4th battalion Cheshire Regiment (2ad Cheshire Militia), on completing his 5 years ser- vice. It is understood that his successor will be Captain Martin-Leahe, who has just completed his four years service as adjutant or the Ist battalion, Cheshire Regiment in India to which post he wat. appointed in August 1896. The new 3rd and 4th Battalion Liverpool Regi- .nent are now being rapidly increased in strength, detach m•mts of recruits being sent from the depot at Warrington to join the new units which are located with the 2nd battalion at Dublin. Recruit- ing is fairly brisk in the Bth Regimental District with headquarters at Warrington and there is no difficulty whatever in obtaining men for the new battalions.

It naturally takes some time before a new battalion can become a really effective unit, but complaints are made that the formation not only of the new 3rd and 4th battalions, Liverpool Regi- ment, but also the other new battalions which the authorities commenced to raise several months ago is being carried out in a very slow manner. Not much energy is being evinced in bringing them up to their full strength, though it is well known that with a display of energy the formation of all the new battalions could be quickly completed. The War Office alone is to blame for this. The two new battalions of the Liverpool Regiment look very well on paper and that appears to be all that the PallNlall officials desire. Otherwise with the large namber of men available each of these battalions could now very soon be brought up to their full establishment of eight companies. The appointment of adjutant of the 2nd Cheshire E.V., at St. Helena, which has been vacant several months when Major Turton. R.E. reverted to duty with the corps of the Royal Engineers at the end of last year will shortly be filled, as also will the ad- jutancies of the let and 2nd Cheshire Engineer Volunteer Corps, also now vacant. A large number of Royal Engineer officers will shortly be returning home from South A frica who will be available for the adjutancies of Volunteer Engineer Corps and then the regiments named will have adjutants ap- pointed.

Moss Rose Lodge No. 1099 _National United

Order-of Free Gardeners.

The Quarterly meeting in canaection with the above Ledge was held on Saturday evening Septem- ber,lsth 1930,in the club room Hop Pole Inn,Horse- market-street, there being a pally number of members present. It has been thought for some time chit, the La Ise oaght to make some little recognition to Brother Alfred Healey, the treasurer, for the valuable sirviceslhe has rendered to his Lodge. He has held office inithis capacity for up- wards of thirteen years, and has given satisfaction to all. A few club nights ago a proposition was made:that they present him with a pair of gold spectacles, and it was carried unanimously and the presentation was fixed for Saturday last. Brother James nudge was called upon to make the present- ation, and in doing so he spoke in very high terms of the way in which Bro H.ealey had discharged his duties. He hoped he would appreciate the present not merely for its value but as a token of the high esteem in which he was held by his fellow-members. In his closing remarks he tried to impress on the young members the wisdom and importance of taking an interest in the Lodge affairs. He hoped that they would work with the same fervent spirit that Bro Healey had done and then the Lodge would be more prosperous in future. Bro Thomas Raybould (Trustee) made a few remarks and hoped that Brother Healey would long live to work in t`:eir midst. Bro William H Parkes, secretary, in his remarks said that he was highly pleased at what had been transacted during the evening. lie also thought that they as Lodge members could not have suggested a more suitable presentation. He hoped that they would have the pleasure of seeing Bro Healey in the same office for a goodly number of years. During his term of office over two thou- I sand pounds had passed through his hands, to help the sick and corn tort. the bereaved ones. The trea- surer responded very feelingly and thanked all for the kindness shown to him. He expressed his deep gratitude for the handsome present which he assured them would be much appreciated. He said that all through bis term of office it had been one source of joy to know what , good had heel done and whatVas being done at the present time and he felt assured that if all the members would put in an attendance the same as they had done this evening the Lodge would pro. press more both numerically and financially. Once more he tried to impress upon young members to take a deeper interest in the Lodge affairs. He was proud to say that that was the biggest meeting that had been held since the Lade hal existed. After a pleasant evening the Lodge was brought to a close in the usual manner. BEST BODTS FOE ALL, UN DKR TEE CLOCK.— Avt

LATEST NEWS.

CHINA.

Allies Attacking Pei-Tang. Heavy Cannonading. TABT7, SEPTEMBER 20. The allies attacked the Pei-Tang forts at daybreak on Thursday. Heavy cannonading is proceeding.— Reuter. [Pei-Tang is a little north of Taku, and is on the coast.]

Attack on a Walled Town Sharp Fighting. Five Hundred Chinese Killed.

PEKIN, SEPTEMBER 12.

The German column under General Von Hoepfner was joined at Luikoncba by a squadron of Bengal Lancers, and attacked the walled city of Lianghsion at nine o'clock yesterday morning. The town was full of Boxers and Chinese Imperial troops.7After the German artillery had shelled the town the gates were blown up, and the troops rushed in. Sharp fighting ensued in the streecs. • No quarter was given, and 500 Chinese were killed. The column returned to Pekin after having burned the town. Only one casualty is reported.

THE WAR.

LATEST TELEGRAMS.

Boer Army Broken Up.

Enemy Destroy their Guns. Another Success by Rundle.

The War Office on Thursday afternoon issued the following from Lord Roberts : NEL9PRITIT, WEDNESDAY.

Out of the 3,000 of the enemy who retreated to KoraatilPoort as we advanced from Machadodorp, about 700 have 1 crossed into Portugese territory, and others have deserted in various directions.

The balance are reported to have crossed the Konnti River, and to be occupying spurs of the Lebombo mountain, south of the railway between Portugese territory and the bridge. A general tumult seems to have occured when the enemy recognised the hopelessness of their cause. The" Long Toms" and several field guns, in- cluding those captured from us wore destroyed. There is nothing now left of the B ier army but a few maraudiug bands. One of these which took up a position at Doom- berg, north-east of VVinburg, is being dealt with under Kelly-Kenny's direction. Rundle yesterday attacked from Bronkhorstfontein and captured one gun and over 30 waggons. Knox came across some of the retreating Boers at Klornpje Dooens, and in the skirmish Lieutenant C. F. Vander By!, 16% Lancers, received a severe wound in the arm.

Hart is moving successfully against another of these bands near Potchefstroom. Hildyard reports his arrival as Groetvlei,l6 miles south east of Utrecht. He took three prisoners, and met with very slight opposition. The two officers taken prisoners in the engage- ment near Cypher a short time ago—Lieutenant Spedding, Royal Irish Rifles, and Lieutenant Watson, Derby Mounted lafantry—were released on the 11th September and sent to Ladybrand.tThey reached Bloemfontein on the 16:h inst.

Accident to Lieutenant Sylvanus Reynolds. On Monday, Mrs. Reynolds, of Whatcroft Hall. North- wich, received a communication'from her son, Lieuten- ant Sylvanus Reynolds, of the 22nd Company Cheshire Imperial Yeomanry, stating that he had met with an accident at Prieska, whilst playing at football. During the game he fell to the ground, and being hurt subse- quently proceeded to Capetown to receive medical attention. Here it was found that his wrist bones were fractured. Lieutenant Reynolds is well known in Warrington and district, and is a prominent sportsman, being actively identified with the North Cheshire Hunt. He is also a prominent polo player. Lance-Corporal Broadbent's Adventures. Mrs. Broadbent, of The Hollies, has received another letter from her son, Lance Corporal J W Broadbent, of which the following are extracts. It is dated " Dra.ghoenda, August 21st . . We got orders at 7.30 to saddle up at nine for a patrol, and took swen days' rations in a waggon, with oedera to take only one blanket —a bit hard after being accustomed to three in a tent. No one knew where we were going till we started, and then we found we were bound for Kheis. Twenty-five of us went, and Captain Daniel and Lieu- tenant Massey and 18 native scouts and their Eaglish officer. We started at ten, and marched till 2.30, 'when we got to awful water and a swamp, and had dinner. Starting again at five, we marched till eight, when we stopped, :having done about 27 miles in the day. We camped on rocky ground, no water we made tea with the water from our water bottles —lt was cald at night. Next morning we started at 6.30 without food, and marched twelve miles to Kheis, arriving there at eleven, where we had breakfast. It was a hot ride and heavy going. I was rear-guard with the waggon. During theme two daes we did not see a single house. When we got to Kheis we went down a steep bank to the river, and forded through to an island. It was RO deep that some of the ponies had to swim,when we got on to the island (from whence Nlajor Orr Erring and others had been shat) we had to go through another ford to grt to Kheis village ; there we found all the houses blown up or burnt down, and only heaps of stones left, and marks, on the trees where the shells had struck. We found shrapnel bullet native cartridges and a burst shell. We had ,breakfast and a bathe, and rested the ponies for the remainder of the day. Not a man was to be seen, Colonel Ad ye had indeed made a clean sweep! In the evening Captain Daniel and two men rode 30 miles to see a relyd„ hut did no business. Next day we left at ten, and after riding ten miles we found two men sneaking in the hills, took them prisoners, and brought them in. One of them was the chief rebel we were sent after. We got his twelve ponies and 1,000 head of sheep and goats. In the afternoon we did twelve more miles. The river was very pretty and rocky. At five we arrived at another place where there was water, and bivouaked for the night. I was on guard with two Schwabes and Lord. Next morning we left at six misaas breakfast, and marched 15 miles. The water where we breakfasted was awful and stagnant. One could not get a cupfull without a lot of fish. Of course, we never drink the water raw, but make cnffee. Next morning we started at eight and marched 15 miles to Koega's Pont, where we bathed and bad dinner. We passed the store where formerly we had our light. There 'Ywere only a few people about including a boy who was bad with heart disease brought on by fright ard wanted to see our " Trooper" Doctor. He was only 15, and had

DEATH OF MR. JOSEPH TITCHETT, WARRINGTON.

Another esteemed Warringtonian passed away on Thursday morning in the person of Mr. Joseph Titchett. estate agent, of Cairo-street, whose death took place at his residence, Mountain Ash Villa, Ellesmere-road, Stockton Heath. The deceased gentleman, who was 58 years of age, had not felt well for some little time, and on Saturday went to Blackpool to recuperate. His health, however, did not improve, and he returned home on Tuesday. He was able to go about the house antil Wednes- day evening, when his condition became such that Dr. Harcourt was summoned. He saw him at nine o'clock. Nothing serious was anticipated, and the deceased gentleman spoke about walk- ing to the doctor's house the bllovving day. Later in the night he became seriously ill, and the doctor was again sent for, but death took place be- fore his arrival. The cause of death was:acute dila- tation of the stomach and cardiac failure. The deceased gentleman was a native of Warrington and was the oldest estate agent in the town. He was also connected with the Mersey Bedstead Works before the formation of the com- pany, and upon that event taking place he joined the board of directors. He took a con- siderable interest in the business, and during recent years devoted most of his time to it. rhe promoter and secretary of the three local Starr-Bowkett Building Societies,he had the utmost confidence of the directors and members. He did not take any prominent part in public affairs, although he did at one time unsuccessfully try to obtain a seat on the Town Council. -le attended the Primitive Metho- dist Chapel, and was treasurer for many year. In politics he Was a staunch Liberal, and was widely esteemed and respected. The -Tumoral will take place at Hill Cliffe this afternoon.

"Coal and Iron."

Mr. F. G. Ruddock, F.1.C., one of the teachers of the Technical Instruction Classes at the School of Science, delivered a lecture at the Museum, War- rington, on Monday evening, on "Coal and Iron." There was a satisfactory attendance, a good num- ber of young men being present. The lecture was- illustrated by experiments and diagrams. The LECTURER, at the outset,saiShe did not pro- pose to attempt to describe why coal had so largely increased in cost but he wanted to go back a long way and say something about the origin of coal and some of the difficulties that arose before coal could be used in the manufacture of iron and steel as et present. It was only within the last 100 years or sa that coal had been used to any great extent: although it had been known for a longer time than that; indeed the Romans used coal in this country' The lecturer then proceeded to explain what grae meant by combustion. Heat was caused by the sub- stance that was being burnt combininr, with another substance, generally the oxygen in the air. Com- bustion was really a chemical combination, gener- ally between two or three substances.. After explaining the difference between exothermic heel's and endothermic heat when heat was absorbed the lecturer showed the enormous power of coal. kit explained that if it were possible to make use or all the energy stored in one lb of coal we should have a power equal to that of 51-; horse power. What use were we making of this power? It had been lying under our feet for ages, and yet it wat.„a comparatively short time since we used it at all. uP to the end of last century coal was not used for the manufacture of iron, now it was used more for the& purpose than any other. Blast furnaces were the great users of coal, but now it only required 16.cwre of coal for the production of one ton of pig ir°,.° whereas it took three tons about 50 years ago. 15 was just about 300 years ago since DudleYr son or a former Earl of Dudley, succeeded in using coal for the production of iron, in place of char- coal. He was successful from, the begin- ning though the- quantity of coal used vrill only three tons a week. Dudley refuse to divulge his secret and was refused any patent rights. In 1740 the iron made in Great Britain mill came to about 17,900 tons, and still no coal wag used, and so on to the end of the century; while st. the present time in the United Kingdom about 91 million tons of pig iron were produced annually Germany produced 7,009,000 ant tt;e United Stfires, 14,000,000. Those were the figures for 1899: AV the present time we were producing in this colintl about 220,000,000 tons of coal annually, the Unite States were producing about the same. whileGtehr; Ger- many was credited with 130,000,000; was-colti only source of power, and were they to "n-`'inue to use it at the present rate? Mr. Ruddock alluded to the use of water gee. He explained the reas% of Thompsons Fox's failure in the Use of water Wl_ and proceeded to show how the difficulties VlThearl: now being overcome. THE RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL OF RUNCORN. PRIVATE STREET WORKS ACT, 1892. NOTICE IS lIEREBY GIVEN, that the RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL of RUNCORN, being the Sanitary Authority for the Rural District of Runcorn. have in pursuance of the Private Street Works Act, 1692, at a Meeting held on the 11th day of September; 1920, by resolu- tion approved of certain Specifications. Plans, and Sections, Bstimates and Provisional Apportionments, prepared by their Surveyor in respect of GASKELL-STREET,LEONARD- STREET, and BOSTOCEI-STREET, in the Contributory Place of Stockton Heath in the said District, and that such resolution is in the following terms, that is to say : (a) That the Rural District Council of Ruucorn, being the Sanitary Authority for the Rural District off Runcorn, level, pave, metal, flag, channel, and make goad, Gaskell-etteet, Leonard-street, and Bostock- street, in the Parish of Stockton Heath in the said District, under the provisions of the Private Street Works Act. 1892. (b) That the Specifications, Plans, Seetions, Estimates and Provisional Apportionments vrepared and submitted by the Surveyor in respect of the said streets be and are hereby approved. (0) That it be, and is, hereby ordered and deslared that the expenses incurred in executing the aforesaid Private Street Works, shall ha payable by equal annual instalments extending over a period of ten years, with interest at the rate of £4 per centum per annum. (d) That application be made to the Local Government Board for their sanction to a loan for the required amount. AND NOTICE IS HEREBY FURTHER GIVEN, that the approved Specifications. Plans, and Sections. Estimates and Provisional Apportionments (or copies thereof certified by the Surveyor) will be kept deposited at 71. High-street. Runcorn, the Office of the Rural District Council of Runcorn, and will be open to inspection at all reasonable times for a period of one month from the 17th day of SEPTEMBER, 190C,during which period any owner of premises affeeted:may In pursuance of Section 7 of the said Act, by written notice served on the said Council, object to the proposals of the said Council on any of the grounds in such section specified. Dated this 14th day of September, 1900. GEORGE F. ASHTON. Clerk of the Rural District Council of Runcorn. 71, High-street, Runcorn.

SUPERANNUATION. Mr. T. HIPWELL (Seherd) moved the following resolu- tion :—" That this meeting is of opinion that it is in tne best interests of the public service that school atten- dance should come under the provisions of the Poor Law Officers' Superannuation Act, 1896,an d this meeting respectfully asks the School Attendance Committee of the Warrington Town Council ti use its influence on behalf of the Local Authorities' Officers' Superannu tion , Bill, to extensl the provisions of the aforesaid Act." Mr. BARNES (Leeds) seconded the resolution, and Mr. BEAVER (Bolton) supported , The CrtAIIIMAIS said he was entirely in accord with everything which had been said by previous speakers on the subject, although atteresent he was not prep red to make any specific promise The resolution was carried. ADDRESS BY ME. T. DIRCHALL, J P. Mr. BIRCHALL, J.P., at the request of the Chairman, also addressed the meeting, and said he was pleased to meet the members of the assoeiation because he was being continually brought into contact with some of them, and knew of the very desirable work they did for the benefit reef the community. It PO happened that on two days of the week when he had to attend at the Police Court, scheol attendance cases were brought before them. Many of his magisterial colleagues; did net like to deal with them. Ha knew the difficulties which beset the earnest educationist, and he saw the advan- tages of compulsion in these matters by the magistrates. A great portion of the lower classes of the population— he did not use the term disrespectfully—but the more uneducated and vicious would not educate their children if it were not for compulsion. The magistrates were continually brought face to face with trio fact that children were only sent to school because parent: were forced to send them ; and he, therefore, welcomed most cordially the increased powers that had been given to the authorities by the Act which had just been passed. In his opinion there had been considerable laxity about this matter. What did some people care for fines of a shilling and " Don't do it again" ? They went away from the court with a smile on their faces and repeated the offence of neele't again. Dur- ing the past few months he and his colleaeuea had inflicted the full p-nalty again and again, and now the parents smarted under the heavier penalty, and he hoped they would see different results. (Applause.) There was another section that had to be dealt with, and they were the poor mothers of large families. He often felt a considerable asnount of pain when he bad to inflict penalties upon them, and he had I often to impress upon them the fact that they must raake sacrifices for the good of their ceildren and send them to scheol. In conclusion, Mr. Birchall said he had always bad treat sympathy with the school attendance I officers in their work, but his sympathy increased as years went on ; and he hoped that the present meeting would be of corsiderable advantage in welding the members together mid making them feel the important results at their disposal. (Applause.) THE BENEVOLENT AND ORPHAN FUND ENDOWMENT SCHEME. Mr Jonas ALLEN (Burnley) introduced the autiect of the Benevolent and Orphan Fund Endowment Scheme. The objects were to make loans in case of necessity to School Bard officers and widows, and grants to orphans He proposed :—" That this meeting is of opinion that the Benevolent and Orphan Fund is at present on an unsatisfactory basis,and that it author:zes the committee of the Federation to draw up a scheme whereby they can place the fund en a proper and a more- substantial basis." Mr JOSEPH COOPER (Farnworth Belten) seconded, and the motion was agreed to, the CUAIRMAN remarking that he hoped the discussion on the matter would lead to something good. EXTENSION OF THE ORGANIZATION. Mr. W. HARPER (Oldham) delivered it short speech en "The importance of extending our organization." He admitted that they were progressing, but hardly at a Satisfactory var.. Mr. JOSEPH COOPER supported the resolution. The motion was carried. THE NEXT CONFERENCE. It was decided, on the motion of the SECRETARY, that the next conference be hell in Maechester. VOTES OF THANKS. Mr. BROMILOW (St. [Plena) moved a vote of thanks to the Ceairman and the other members of the Warring- ton Committee. Mr. JONES (Manchester) seconded, and the motion was earrieci The CHAIRMAN said it had been a swat pleasure to him to be present. He had learnt many thine; it was desirable for him to know as a member of the School Attendanee Committee, THE MAYOR ON THE VALUE OF EDUCATION. Mr. LONGWORTEI (Bolton) proposed a vote of thanks to the Mayor for allowing the members the use of the rown Hall. Mr. Fluorin (Manchester) seconded, and the motion Was heartily agreed to. The MAYOR, ache had jest arrived was received with applause, and said he wrs extremelc obliged t a thoee present for the very hearty vote of thanks which had been nesaed to him. He must express his very deep re- gret that he Was compelled through circumatanc; ir of a somewhat personal character to break the engagement which he had made to be with them fer the whole of the afternoon. The feet of the matter was that they in Warrington ev,rri beceming a little bit conceited, and they had an idea that whatever functions the bigger cities had they ought not to forget that midway between Manchester and Liverpool thsre was a very important town called Warrington, and that it should he asked through its repre- sentatives to take part in those functions. THAT HOPE DEFERRED MAKETH THE 1 HEART SICK is just as true now as ever it was will be attested by all whose most cherished aspirations have remained unfulfilled. In every walk of life we encounter this hankering after the (supposed) unattainable, whilst in many cases the remedy is close at hand if those interested only knew how to avail themselves of it. It affects most businesses, the furnishing trade especially. For instance, a young man meets a maiden fair whose appearance and merits he approves. She, likewise, appreciates his qualities, and quickly an affection springs up between them. How pleasant they think it would be to marry and start housekeeping together. Then the stumbling block presents itself—where is the cash to purchase furniture to come from? With him, time past, like with most young fellows, "enough for the day has been the evil thereof." He has savei practically nothing so the marriage is postponed indefinitely. Now, this is where PITTS S step in with a timely suggestion. Instead of deferring the hope and making the hearts sick why not, by a fair and equitable arrangement, defer the payments and make both hearts glad? THE TECHNICAL ing subjects :—Shorthand. book-keeping, English lite ra- ture,commercial geography, French, Spanish and Latin. INSTITUTE. There will also be institute and other scholarships. Two prizes are also offered to students taking the commercial course. TEIE CONTINUATION SCHOOL. The Continuation School (People's C ollege,Arpley-st.,) provides for young persons of both sexes tbe means of continuing their education after leaving the day school, and forms a sPries of preparatory classes introductory to those in the Schools of Art and Science. Students who, having left school some time, wish to recover lost ground in the ordinary subjects before taking up new ones will find these classes of very great assistance. Beginners generally are strongly recommended to join the Coatin- uation School for at least a session before proceeding further. A separate class in ordinary subjects for adult , students will be formed. School prizes for regular attendance will be given at the end of the session, according to the following rule :—Attendance en 50 full evenings—prize value 3s 61. Attendance on 35 full evenings—prize value 2s 6.i. Echolarships, entitling the bolder to remission of fees in the next session will be awarded on the result of the examinations held in the spring; Prizes on the result of the same examina- tion are given by the Union of Lancashire and Cheshire Institutes. Students who pass in drawing will receive , free admission to the School of Art for one session. ' Fee : 2s 63 a term (13 weeks), or 3d per week.—Each student entering is required to attend regularly in at least two subjects. All fees are payable in advance, to the superintendent at the school. Classes will commence on the 17th. There will be two weeks' holidays at Christmas, and the school will close at Easter. LECTURES. The usual opening and holiday lectures will be delivered. A lecture illustrated with experiments, will be delived in the lecture room, Museum-street, at eight o'clock, on Monday evening next by Mr F G Ruddock, F.1.C., on" Coal and Iron.' At the first meeting of the book-keeping classes at People's College on Wednesday next, Mr G F Clarke, A.S.A.A., will deliver a !cetera on " The Prieciples of Book-keeping." A course of Univer- sity Extension Lectures on " Victorian Poets." will be commenced on October 3rd by Mr E T Campagnac,M. A Two holiday lectures on " Sound and the Telephone . will be delivered in the Lecture room (Museum-street) by Mr J R Ashworth, M.S.C., lecturer on physics, shortly after Christmas.

THE CRISIS IN CHINA • has caused much alarm. A few yeare ago it would have affected the comfort of every borne in England by increasing the cost of tea. To-day. however, with the delightfully fragrant and refreshing British-grown tea which is being shipped weekly to BROOKE BOND & Co., LTD., from India and Ceylon, tea-drinkers may regard the crisis with equanimity. Thanks to British capital and industry, India and Ceylon have become the great tea-gardens of the world, and we are no longer dependent on the long-eyed race who once held the tea monopoly. Although the capital invested in British tea-production amounts to over £14,000,000, there is no tea more highly prized than BROoKE. BOWS. It is obtained from estates in the finest stage of eultiYation, is the choicest pick of each season, and is always exquisite and delicious. And yet the Nimble Ninepence Buys Half-a-Pound, and each packet is full weight without the paper ! 30,000 AGENTS SELL IT AT 1/6 PER LB. BROOKE, BOND'S TEA.
